Well-posedness of stochastic heat equation with distributional drift and skew stochastic heat equation
Siva Athreya, Oleg Butkovsky, Khoa Lê, Leonid Mytnik

Abstract
We study stochastic reaction–diffusion equation ∂ t u t ​ ( x ) = 1 2 ​ ∂ x ​ x 2 u t ​ ( x ) + b ⁡ ( u t ​ ( x ) ) + W ˙ t ​ ( x ) , t > 0 , x ∈ D \partial_{t}u_{t}(x)=\frac{1}{2}\partial^{2}_{xx}u_{t}(x)+b(u_{t}(x))+\dot{W}_{t}(x),\quad t>0,\,x\in{D} where b b is a generalized function in the Besov space ℬ q , ∞ β ​ ( ℝ ) \mathcal{B}^{\beta}_{q,\infty}(\mathbb{R}) , D ⊂ ℝ {D}\subset\mathbb{R} and W ˙ \dot{W} is a space-time white noise on ℝ + × D \mathbb{R}_{+}\times{D} . We introduce a notion of a solution to this equation and obtain existence and uniqueness of a strong solution whenever β − 1 / q ⩾ − 1 \beta-1/q\geqslant-1 , β > − 1 \beta>-1 and q ∈ [ 1 , ∞ ] q\in[1,\infty] . This class includes equations with b b being measures, in particular, b = δ 0 b=\delta_{0} which corresponds to the skewed stochastic heat equation. For β − 1 / q > − 3 / 2 \beta-1/q>-3/2 , we obtain existence of a weak solution. Our results extend the work of Bass and Chen (2001) to the framework of stochastic partial differential equations and generalize the results of Gyöngy and Pardoux (1993) to distributional drifts. To establish these results, we exploit the regularization effect of the white noise through a new strategy based on the stochastic sewing lemma introduced in Lê (2020).



1
Introduction

While regularization by noise for ordinary differential equations (ODEs) is quite well understood by now, much less is known about regularization by noise for partial differential equations (PDEs). The goal of this article is to analyze regularization by noise for parabolic PDEs and to build new robust techniques for studying this phenomenon. We consider stochastic heat equation with a drift (stochastic reaction–diffusion equation)

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\partial_{t}u_{t}(x)=\frac{1}{2}\partial^{2}_{xx}u_{t}(x)+b(u_{t}(x))+\dot{W}_{t}(x),\quad t\in(0,T_{0}],\,x\in{D},\\
&u(0,x)=u_{0}(x),\end{aligned}\right.

(1.1)

where
b
is a generalized function in the Besov space
\mathcal{B}^{\beta}_{q,\infty}(\mathbb{R},\mathbb{R})
,

\beta\in\mathbb{R}
,
q\in[1,\infty]
, the domain
{D}
is either
{[0,1]}
or
\mathbb{R}
,
T_{0}>0
,

\dot{W}
is space-time white noise on
[0,T_{0}]\times{D}
, and

u_{0}:{D}\to\mathbb{R}
is a bounded measurable function. Note that for
\beta<0
this equation is not well-posed in the standard sense: indeed in this case
b
is not a function but only a distribution and thus the composition
b(u_{t}(x))
is a priori not well-defined. We introduce a natural notion of a solution to this equation in the spirit of
[
BC01
, Definition 2.1]
. We show that equation (
1.1
) has a unique strong solution if
\beta-\frac{1}{q}\geqslant-1
,
\beta>-1
and
q\in[1,\infty]
, see
2.6
. This includes equations where
b
is measure, in particular, the skewed stochastic heat equation, which corresponds to the case
b=\kappa\delta_{0}
,
\kappa\in\mathbb{R}
. The latter equation is important for the stochastic interface models and appeared in
[
BZ14
]
where its well-posedness was left open. We resolve this problem in our paper, see
2.8
and
2.9
.

Our results extend
[
BC01
]
to the framework of stochastic partial differential equations (SPDEs) and generalize
[
GP93a
,
GP93b
]
to singular drifts. We exploit the regularization effect of the white noise and develop a new proof strategy based on stochastic sewing
[
Lê20
]
.
Furthermore, we give several extensions of the stochastic sewing lemma which allow singularities, critical exponents and usage of random controls. In particular, we extend to the stochastic setting deterministic sewing with controls, see, e.g.,
[
Lyo98
,
Gub04
,
FdLP06
,
FZ18
]
. We would like to stress that in contrast to vast majority of regularization-by-noise papers for ODEs
[
Ver80
,
HS81
,
Dav07b
,
CG16
,
ZZ17
]
our method uses neither Girsanov transform nor Zvonkin transformation. These two popular techniques are not useful in our setting.

It has been known since 1970s that ill-posed deterministic systems can become well-posed if a random noise is injected into the system. Consider the following simple example. The ODE
dX_{t}=b(X_{t})dt
with a bounded measurable vector field
b:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
is ill-posed. It might have infinitely many or no solutions in some specific cases. Yet, if this deterministic system is perturbed by a Brownian noise
B
, then the corresponding stochastic differential equation (SDE)

dX_{t}=b(X_{t})\,dt+dB_{t},\,\,X_{0}=x_{0}

(1.2)

is well-posed and has a unique strong solution
[
Zvo74
,
Ver80
]
. This phenomenon is called
regularization by noise
, see
[
Fla11
]
.

Regularization by noise for ODEs has been studied extensively since the pioneering works of Zvonkin and Veretennikov mentioned above and many interesting results are available by now. Strong existence and uniqueness of solutions to (
1.2
) for the case of possibly unbounded drifts satisfying only a certain integrability condition was proved by Krylov and Röckner in
[
KR05
]
, see also related works
[
Zha05
,
FGP10
,
FF11
]
. Well-posedness of
(
1.2
) in a stronger sense (path–by–path uniqueness) for the case of bounded
b
was obtained in the celebrated paper
[
Dav07b
]
of Davie.

Furthermore, it turned out that equation (
1.2
) makes sense even if
b
is a distribution. In this case, the term
b(X_{t})
is not defined and one has to define a notion of a solution to this equation. These can be done in a number of ways. Zhang and Zhao
[
ZZ17
]
proved weak existence and uniqueness of solutions to (
1.2
) for
b
belonging to the Besov-Hölder space
\mathcal{B}_{\infty,\infty}^{\alpha}
with
\alpha>-1/2
. Bass and Chen
[
BC01
]
established strong existence and uniqueness of solutions to (
1.2
) under the same assumptions in dimension one. A critical case when
b
is the spatial white noise in one dimension is treated by Hu et al.
[
HLM17
]
. The specific case of
b=\kappa\delta_{0}
,
|\kappa|\leqslant 1
, which corresponds to the skew Brownian motion, was treated by Harisson and Shepp in
[
HS81
]
and extended by Le Gall in
[
LG84
]
.

Let us mention that there is nothing special here about
B
being a Gaussian or a Markov process: indeed, regularization for ODEs driven by various other noise processes (Lévy noise, fractional Brownian noise) also holds, see, e.g.,
[
Pri12
,
CG16
,
KP20
,
HP20
,
Lê20
]
.

Unfortunately, most of the methods which are used in the ODE/SDE setting are not transferable to the PDE setting. Indeed, the most popular technique, the Zvonkin-Veretennikov transform
[
Zvo74
,
Ver80
,
FF11
,
Pri12
]
, allows to pass from the analysis of the original SDE with irregular drift, to the analysis of a new SDE (called sometimes “virtual equation”
[
FIR17
]
) whose drift and diffusion are easier to handle. Then well-posedness for the original SDE can be derived from the well-posedness of the new SDE.
However, to implement this technique, it is absolutely essential that the stochastic system has a good Itô formula. While the Itô formula is also available for SPDEs (
[
Zam06
,
Bel20
]
), it involves additional renormalized non-linear terms. This makes its application very difficult.

An alternative strategy was suggested in
[
CG16
,
GG20
]
, in which the authors work directly with the original SDE, fixing a trajectory of the noise and viewing the equation as a non-linear Young equation. The Girsanov theorem is pivotal to the whole approach: if the noise does not allow for a good version of Girsanov theorem, the obtained results are not optimal, see
[
GG20
, Lemma 10 and Remark 18]
.
It seems challenging to extend this method to SPDEs with distributional drifts. One particular problem is that the the domain of the non-linear vector field is no longer of finite dimension but is a certain function space.

Thus, it is clear that the analysis of regularization-by-noise for PDEs requires a very different approach. One of the first results in this research area belongs to
Gyöngy and Pardoux
[
GP93a
,
GP93b
]
. The authors used comparison theorems to establish existence and uniqueness of (analytically) weak solutions to (
1.1
) for the case where the drift
b
is the sum of a bounded function and an
L_{q}
-integrable function with
q>2
. Path–by–path uniqueness of solutions to (
1.1
) for bounded
b
was obtained recently in
[
BM19
]
.

Bounebache and Zambotti
[
BZ14
]
considered stochastic partial differential equations with measure valued drift. In particular, motivated by problems arising in the study of random interface models, see, e.g.,
[
Fun05
]
, they studied the
skew stochastic heat equation
, i.e., (
1.1
) with
b

being a Dirac delta function. Using
Dirichlet form techniques, they obtained existence of a weak
solution. However existence and uniqueness of strong solution remained open.
Resolving this problem was one of the motivations for our work.

From the above discussion, we note that there is a gap between
the ODE and PDE settings. For ODEs numerous results treating
distributional drifts are available

[
HS81
,
BC01
,
CG16
,
ZZ17
,
PvZ20
]
. On the other hand, almost no such
results were known for PDEs, note though the paper
[
GP18
]
and the discussion there.
Our goal in this article is to construct a robust general method for proving strong
existence and uniqueness to (
1.1
) in the case where the drift

b
is a Schwarz distribution. In particular, we treat the skew stochastic heat
equation.

Inspired by the finite dimensional setting
[
BC01
,
CG16
]
we define a
natural notion of a solution to (
1.1
) in
2.3
and
show that (
1.1
) has a unique strong solution when
b
belongs
to the Besov space
\mathcal{B}^{\beta}_{q,\infty}(\mathbb{R},\mathbb{R})
,

\beta-\frac{1}{q}\geqslant-1
,
\beta>-1
and
q\in[1,\infty]
, see

2.6
, and when
b
is a finite Radon measure,

2.8
. We also prove strong convergence of smooth
approximations to (
1.1
) in
2.10
. We establish strong existence and uniqueness of the skew stochastic heat equation in
2.9
and
show that this equation appears naturally as a certain scaling limit of
“standard” SPDEs where the drifts are continuous integrable functions, see

2.12
.

To obtain these results we develop a new strategy based on certain regularization
estimates for SPDEs, see
5.2
. These estimates can be viewed as infinite-dimensional analogues of the corresponding Davie’s bounds for SDEs
[
Dav07b
, Proposition 2.1]
, see also
[
ZZ17
, Lemma 5.8]
. Note though that Davie’s method involves exact moment computations and is not easily extended to the SPDE setting. Therefore to obtain these regularization estimates, we extend and employ the stochastic sewing technique introduced originally in
[
Lê20
]
. We believe that these new stochastic sewing lemmas (
4.1
,
4.5
and
4.7
) form a very useful toolkit which might be of independent interest. The usage of regularization estimates are explained briefly in
Section

2.2
.

We conclude the introduction by commenting on the optimality of our results. It is known
[
LN09
]
that for each fixed space point, the free stochastic heat equation (that is, equation (
1.1
) with
b\equiv 0
) behaves “qualitatively” like a fractional Brownian motion (fBM) with the Hurst parameter
1/4
, denoted further by
B^{1/4}
. Therefore, one can expect that strong existence and uniqueness for equation (
1.1
) would hold under the same conditions on
b
as in the equation

dX_{t}=b(X_{t})\,dt+\,dB^{1/4}_{t},

that is
b\in\mathcal{B}^{\beta}_{q}
, where
\beta-1/q>-1
,
[
CG16
, Theorem 1.13]
. This indeed turned out to be the case, see
2.6
, even though the method of
[
CG16
]
could not be transferred to the PDE setting.
Note that this class of functions does not include the Dirac delta function, which lies in
\mathcal{B}^{-1+1/q}_{q}
,
q\in[1,\infty]
. Therefore we had to come up with an additional argument to cover the case
\beta-1/q=-1
as well (see
Proposition

3.6
).

The rest of the paper is organized as follows. We present our main results and a brief overview of the proof strategy in
Section

2
. Since the proofs are quite technical, for the convenience of the reader we split them in several steps. In

Section

3
we prove the main results. The proofs are based on key propositions, which are stated also in
Section

3
and proved in
Section

5
. Extensions of the stochastic sewing lemma are stated and proved in
Section

4
. The proofs of crucial regularity results are given in
Section

6
.

Appendices

A
,
B
and
C
contains auxiliary technical results which we will use freely throughout the paper.

Convention on constants.

Throughout the paper
C
denotes a positive constant whose value may
change from line to line. All other constants will be denoted by
C_{1},C_{2},\ldots
. They are all positive and their precise values are not
important. The dependence of constants on parameters if needed will be indicated, e.g,
C_{\beta}
or
C(\beta)
.

2
Main results

2.1
Well-posedness of the stochastic heat equation with a distributional drift

We begin by introducing the necessary notation. Let

{\mathbf{B}}({D})
be the space of all real bounded measurable functions on
{D}
.
Let

\mathcal{C}_{b}^{\infty}=\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{b}({D},\mathbb{R})
be the space of infinitely
differentiable real functions on
{D}
which are bounded and have bounded
derivatives. We denote by
\mathcal{C}_{c}^{\infty}=\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{c}({D},\mathbb{R})
the set of functions in
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{b}({D},\mathbb{R})
with compact supports. For
\beta\in(0,1]
, let
\mathcal{C}^{\beta}
be
the space of bounded Hölder continuous functions with exponent
\beta
.
For each
\beta\in\mathbb{R}
and
q\in[1,\infty]
, let
\mathcal{B}^{\beta}_{q}
denote the (nonhomogeneous) Besov space
\mathcal{B}^{\beta}_{q,\infty}(\mathbb{R})
of regularity
\beta
and integrability
q
, see
Definition

A.1
. We recall that
for
\beta\in(0,1)
, the space
\mathcal{B}^{\beta}_{\infty}

coincides with the space
\mathcal{C}^{\beta}
(see
[
BCD11
, page 99]
).
For
\beta\in(-1,0)
the space
\mathcal{B}^{\beta}_{\infty}
includes all derivatives (in the distributional
sense) of functions in
\mathcal{C}^{\beta+1}
.

Let
g_{t}
,
p_{t}^{{per}}
,
p_{t}^{{Neu}}
be the free–space heat kernel, the heat kernel on
{[0,1]}
with periodic boundary conditions, and the heat kernel on
{[0,1]}
with the Neumann boundary conditions, respectively. That is,

\displaystyle g_{t}(x):=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi t}}e^{-\frac{x^{2}}{2t}},\quad t>0,\,x\in\mathbb{R};

(2.1)

\displaystyle p_{t}^{per}(x,y):=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}g_{t}(x-y+n),\quad t>0,\,x,y\in{[0,1]};

(2.2)

\displaystyle p_{t}^{Neu}(x,y):=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}(g_{t}(x-y+2n)+g_{t}(x+y+2n)),\quad t>0,\,x,y\in{[0,1]}.

(2.3)

Our main results are valid in three different setups: when equation (
1.1
) is considered on the domain
D=\mathbb{R}
; when (
1.1
) is considered on the domain
D={[0,1]}
with the periodic boundary conditions; and when (
1.1
) is considered on
D={[0,1]}
with the Neumann boundary conditions. To simplify the notation and to ease the stating of the results we will use the notation
p
for
g
,
p^{per}
or
p^{Neu}
and
D
will denote the corresponding domain.

Assumption 2.1
.

From now on if not stated otherwise the pair
(D,p)
will stand for one of the three options:
(\mathbb{R},g)
,
({[0,1]},p^{per})
, or
({[0,1]},p^{Neu})
.

The specific choice of the domain and of the boundary condition (out of the above three options) will not affect the results and arguments in most places of the paper. In very few places of the paper where the choice of the domain is important we will highlight it.

For bounded measurable functions
\varphi\colon{D}\to\mathbb{R}
,
t>0
we put

\displaystyle P_{t}\varphi(x):=\int_{{D}}p_{t}(x,y)\varphi(y)\,dy,\quad x\in{D}.

It will be convenient to denote the heat semigroup on
\mathbb{R}
by

\displaystyle G_{t}\psi(x):=\int_{\mathbb{R}}g_{t}(x-y)\psi(y)\,dy,\quad x\in\mathbb{R},t>0,

for all bounded measurable functions
\psi\colon\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
.

Let
T_{0}>0
and let
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},(\mathcal{F}_{t})_{t\in[0,T_{0}]},\mathsf{P})
be a filtered probability space.
For each
m\in[1,\infty]
, the norm of a random variable
\xi
in
L_{m}(\Omega)
is denoted by

\|\xi\|_{{L_{m}}}
. Here, as usual, we use the convention
\|\xi\|_{L_{\infty}}:=\esssup_{\omega\in\Omega}|\xi(\omega)|
when
m=\infty
.
We recall that a random process
W\colon L_{2}({D},dx)\times[0,T_{0}]\times\Omega\to\mathbb{R}
is called
(\mathcal{F}_{t})
-white noise
if for any
\varphi\in L_{2}({D},dx)
the process
(W_{t}(\varphi))_{t\in[0,T_{0}]}
is an
(\mathcal{F}_{t})
–Brownian motion with
\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057ptW_{t}(\varphi)^{2}=t\|\varphi\|^{2}_{L_{2}({D},dx)}

and
W_{t}(\varphi)
and
W_{t}(\psi)
are independent whenever
\varphi,\psi\in{L_{2}}({D},dx)
with
\int_{D}\varphi(x)\psi(x)\,dx=0
.

Set now

V_{t}(x):=\int_{0}^{t}\int_{{D}}p_{t-r}(x,y)W(dr,dy),\quad t\geqslant 0,\,x\in{D},

(2.4)

where the integration in (
2.4
) is a stochastic
integration understood in the sense of Walsh (
[
Wal86
, Chapter 2]
).
It is known that
V
is a Gaussian random field adapted to
(\mathcal{F}_{t})
and has a continuous version on
[0,T_{0}]\times{D}
which we will use throughout the paper.
It follows from (
2.4
) that
V
has the local nondeterminism property

\|V_{t}(x)-\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt(V_{t}(x)|\mathcal{F}_{s})\|_{L_{2}}\geqslant\pi^{-\frac{1}{4}}|t-s|^{\frac{1}{4}}\quad\textrm{for every}\quad s\leqslant t\quad\textrm{and}\quad x\in{D}.

(2.5)

Let us note that we do not analyze in our article equation (
1.1
) equipped with the Dirichlet boundary conditions.
In this case, the right-hand side of (
2.5
) goes to
0
as
x
tends to the boundary of the domain.

The uniformity in
x
in (
2.5
) plays a key role in our arguments. While it is possible to adapt our proofs to treat Dirichlet boundary condition as well (and we are convinced that our results hold in the setting), we have deliberately decided not to focus on this case in order to emphasize how our approach works and to avoid additional technical difficulties.

Now let us give a notion of a solution to (
1.1
). It is inspired by the definition in finite dimensional setting in
[
BC01
, Definition 2.1]
.

Definition 2.2
.

Let
f
be a distribution in
\mathcal{B}^{\beta}_{q}
with
\beta\in\mathbb{R}
and
q\in[1,\infty]
. We say that a sequence of functions
(f_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}}
converges to
f
in
\mathcal{B}^{\beta-}_{q}
as
n\rightarrow\infty
if
\sup_{n\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}}\|f_{n}\|_{\mathcal{B}^{\beta}_{q}}<\infty
and

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\|f_{n}-f\|_{\mathcal{B}^{\beta^{\prime}}_{q}}=0,\quad\text{for any $\beta^{\prime}<\beta$}.

It is clear that for any
f\in\mathcal{B}^{\beta}_{q}
, there is a sequence of functions
(f_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}}\subset\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{b}
which converges to
f
in
\mathcal{B}^{\beta-}_{q}
as
n\to\infty
. For example, one can take
f_{n}:=G_{1/n}f
, see
Lemma

A.3
.

Definition 2.3
.

Let
\beta\in\mathbb{R}
,
q\in[1,\infty]
,
b\in\mathcal{B}_{q}^{\beta}
and
T_{0}>0
. Let
u_{0}\in{\mathbf{B}}({D})
. A measurable adapted process
u\colon(0,T_{0}]\times{D}\times\Omega\to\mathbb{R}
is called a
solution of (
1.1
) with initial condition
u_{0}

if there exists a process
K\colon[0,T_{0}]\times{D}\times\Omega\to\mathbb{R}
such that

1.

u_{t}(x)=P_{t}u_{0}(x)+K_{t}(x)+V_{t}(x)
a.s., where
x\in{D}
,
t\in(0,T_{0}]
;

2.

for any sequence of functions
(b^{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}}
in
\mathcal{C}_{b}^{\infty}
converging to
b
in
\mathcal{B}_{q}^{\beta-}

we have for any
N>0

\sup_{t\in[0,T_{0}]}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in{D}\\
|x|\leqslant N\end{subarray}}\left|\int_{0}^{t}\int_{D}p_{t-r}(x,y)b^{n}(u_{r}(y))\,dy\,dr-K_{t}(x)\right|\to 0\quad\text{in probability as $n\to\infty$};

3.

a.s. the function
u
is continuous on
(0,T_{0}]\times{D}
.

We note that
2.3
defines a solution to equation (
1.1
) in three different settings, see
2.1
. When
b\in\mathcal{C}^{\beta}
with
\beta>0
, we can choose a sequence
(b^{n})
which converges to
b
in uniformly. Then it is immediate that
2.3
is equivalent to the usual notion of a mild solution of (
1.1
), that is,
\mathsf{P}
-almost surely

u_{t}(x)=P_{t}u_{0}(x)+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{D}p_{t-r}(x,y)b(u_{r}(y))dydr+V_{t}(x)\quad\forall(t,x)\in[0,T_{0}]\times{D}.

(Eq(
u_{0};b
))

We say that a solution
u\equiv\{u_{t}(x):t\in(0,T_{0}],x\in{D}\}
is a strong solution to (
1.1
) if
it is adapted to the filtration
(\mathcal{F}^{W}_{t})
. A weak solution of
(
1.1
) is a couple
(u,W)
on a complete filtered probability
space
(\Omega,{\cal G},(\mathcal{G}_{t})_{t\geqslant 0},\mathsf{P})
such that
u
is adapted to
({\cal G}_{t})
,
W
is
({\cal G}_{t})
-white noise, and
u
is a solution
to (
1.1
). We say that strong uniqueness holds
for (
1.1
) if whenever
u
and
\widetilde{u}
are two strong solutions
of (
1.1
) defined on the same probability space with the same initial condition

u_{0}
, then

\mathsf{P}(u_{t}(x)=\widetilde{u}_{t}(x)\mbox{ for all }t\in(0,T_{0}],\,\,x\in{D})=1.

Consider the following class of solutions.

Definition 2.4
.

Let
\kappa\in[0,1]
. We say that a solution
u
to SPDE (
1.1
) belongs to the
class
\mathcal{V}(\kappa)
if for any
m\geqslant 2
,
\sup_{(t,x)\in(0,T_{0}]\times{D}}\|u_{t}(x)\|_{L_{m}}<\infty
and

\sup_{0<s\leqslant t\leqslant T_{0}}\sup_{x\in{D}}\frac{\|u_{t}(x)-V_{t}(x)-(P_{t-s}[u_{s}-V_{s}](x))\|_{L_{m}}}{|t-s|^{\kappa}}<\infty.

Remark 2.5
.

Recalling that
u_{t}=P_{t}u_{0}+K_{t}+V_{t}
,
t>0
, we see that the numerator in
2.4
is just
\|K_{t}(x)-P_{t-s}K_{s}(x)\|_{L_{m}}
. Thus, class
\mathcal{V}(\kappa)
contains solutions of (
1.1
) such that the moments of their drifts satisfy certain regularity conditions.

We are now ready to present our main result. Fix
T_{0}>0
and recall
2.1
.

Theorem 2.6
.

Let
\beta\in\mathbb{R}
,
q\in[1,\infty]
,
b\in\mathcal{B}_{q}^{\beta}
and
u_{0}\in{\mathbf{B}}(D)
.

1.

If
\beta-\frac{1}{q}>-\frac{3}{2}
, then there exists a weak solution to equation (
1.1
) and this solution is in the class
\mathcal{V}(\kappa)
for
\kappa\in(0,1+\frac{\beta\wedge 0}{4}-\frac{1}{4q}]\setminus\{1\}
.

2.

If
\beta-\frac{1}{q}\geqslant-1
and
\beta>-1
, then in the class
\mathcal{V}(3/4)
there exists a unique strong solution to equation (
1.1
).

Remark 2.7
.

It follows from the proof of
2.6
(see
Proposition

3.6
) that in the case
\beta-\frac{1}{q}\geqslant-1
,
\beta>-1
, condition (2) of
2.3
can be relaxed. Namely, if a measurable adapted process
u\colon(0,T_{0}]\times{D}\times\Omega\to\mathbb{R}
satisfies conditions (1) and (3) of
2.3
, belongs to
\mathcal{V}(3/4)
, and satisfies the following weaker condition (2
′
)

(2
′
)

there exists a sequence of functions
b^{n}\in\mathcal{C}_{b}^{\infty}
converging to
b
in
\mathcal{B}_{q}^{\beta-}
such that for any
t\in[0,T_{0}]
,

x\in{D}
, we have

\int_{0}^{t}\int_{D}p_{t-r}(x,y)b^{n}(u_{r}(y))\,dy\,dr\to K_{t}(x)\,\,\,\,\text{in probability as $n\to\infty$},

then it satisfies a stronger condition (2) of
2.3

for
any
sequence of smooth approximations

b^{n}\to b
in
\mathcal{B}_{q}^{\beta-}
.

Note that the additional assumption in
2.6
(ii) that the solution lies in
\mathcal{V}(3/4)
is a natural extension to the SPDE setting of a very similar condition arising in the analysis of SDEs with the distributional drift. It appears in
[
BC01
, Definition 2.1]
,
[
ZZ17
, Definition 3.1 and Corollary 5.3]
,
[
ABM20
, Theorem 2.3]
,
[
HP20
, Lemma 31]
.

Since for any
q\in[1,\infty]
,
L_{q}(\mathbb{R})
is continuously embedded in
\mathcal{B}^{0}_{q}(\mathbb{R})
(
[
BCD11
, Proposition 2.39]
),
2.6
complements the corresponding results in
[
GP93a
,
GP93b
]
. Namely,
2.6
(ii) allows
L_{1}(\mathbb{R})
-integrable drifts, while the aforementioned papers requires the drift to be
L_{q}(\mathbb{R})
-integrable for some
q>2
. Note that the drift
b
in
[
GP93a
,
GP93b
]
can also depend on
(t,x)
. It is clear that our method can be adapted to this setting; however, for clarity and to highlight the main ideas, we only consider equations of the type (
1.1
) herein.

Since signed measures belong to
\mathcal{B}^{0}_{1}
(
[
BCD11
, Prop. 2.39]
),
2.6
(ii) is also applicable for this class.
Other specific cases of drift
b
for which (
1.1
) has a unique strong solution include
b(u)=|u|^{-\sigma}
,
\sigma\in(-1,0)
and
b(u)=\zeta^{-1}(u)
, where
\zeta^{-1}
is the Cauchy principal value of
1/u
, defined in (
2.9
) below. This is due to the fact that
|\cdot|^{-\sigma}
belongs to
\mathcal{B}^{0}_{1/\sigma}
while
\zeta^{-1}
belongs to
\mathcal{B}^{-1/2}_{2}
(see
Lemma

A.4
).
In the case when
b
is a finite non-negative measure on
\mathbb{R}
, we have the following improved result.

Theorem 2.8
.

Let
b
be a finite non-negative Radon measure. Then for any bounded initial condition
u_{0}
equation (
1.1
) has a unique strong solution.

Corollary 2.9
.

The skew stochastic heat equation, that is equation (
1.1
) with
b=\kappa\delta_{0}
,
\kappa\in\mathbb{R}
, has a unique strong solution for every bounded measurable initial condition
u_{0}
.

Note that in
2.8
and
2.9
, the assumption
u\in\mathcal{V}(3/4)
is not required.

Our next result is a stability theorem.
Let
(b^{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}}
be a smooth approximation of
b
.
2.10
shows that a solution to SPDE (
1.1
) with smooth drift
b^{n}
converges as
n\to\infty
and that the limit does not depend on the particular choice of the approximating sequence.
Such solutions are sometimes called “constructable solutions”
[
GP93b
, Definition 2.2]
. We show that in our setting “constructable solutions” coincide with the standard solutions defined above.

Theorem 2.10
.

Let
\beta\in\mathbb{R}
,
q\in[1,\infty]
. Suppose that

\beta-\frac{1}{q}\geqslant-1
and
\beta>-1
. Let
b\in\mathcal{B}_{q}^{\beta}
,
u_{0}\in{\mathbf{B}}({D})
. Let
(b^{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}}
be a sequence of bounded continuous functions converging in
\mathcal{B}^{\beta-}_{q}
to
b
. Let
(u_{0}^{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}}
be a sequence of functions from
{\mathbf{B}}({D})
converging to
u_{0}
uniformly on
{D}
.

Let
u^{n}
be a strong solution to
Eq(
u_{0}^{n};b^{n}
)
. Then there exists a measurable function
u\colon[0,T_{0}]\times{D}\times\Omega\to\mathbb{R}
such that

1.

for any
N>0
we have

\sup_{t\in(0,T_{0}]}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in{D}\\
|x|\leqslant N\end{subarray}}|u^{n}_{t}(x)-u_{t}(x)|\to 0\quad\text{in probability as $n\to\infty$};

(2.6)

2.

u
is a strong solution to (
1.1
) with the initial condition
u_{0}
;

3.

u
satisfies

\displaystyle\sup_{0<s<t\leqslant T}\sup_{x\in{D}}\esssup_{\omega\in\Omega}\frac{\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt\left(|u_{t}(x)-V_{t}(x)-P_{t-s}(u_{s}-V_{s})(x)|^{m}|\mathcal{F}_{s}\right)^{1/m}}{|t-s|^{3/4}}<\infty

(2.7)

for every
m\geqslant 1
. In particular,
u
belongs to
\mathcal{V}(3/4)
.

Finally, we state two interesting applications of
2.10
. The first one is the comparison principle for the solutions of SPDE (
1.1
), which extends the standard comparison principle. As usual, for two Schwarz distributions
b^{\prime},b^{\prime\prime}
we write
b^{\prime}\preceq b^{\prime\prime}
, if for any
nonnegative
test function
\varphi\in\mathcal{C}_{b}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R},\mathbb{R})
one has
\langle b^{\prime},\varphi\rangle\leqslant\langle b^{\prime\prime},\varphi\rangle
. It is known (see, e.g.,
[
Tre67
, Exersice 22.5]
) that

b^{\prime}\preceq b^{\prime\prime}
if and only if
b^{\prime\prime}-b^{\prime}
is a nonnegative Radon measure.

Corollary 2.11
.

Let
\beta\in\mathbb{R}
,
q\in[1,\infty]
. Suppose that

\beta-\frac{1}{q}\geqslant-1
and
\beta>-1
. Let
b^{\prime},b^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{B}_{q}^{\beta}
,
u^{\prime}_{0},u^{\prime\prime}_{0}\in{\mathbf{B}}({D})
. Let
u^{\prime},u^{\prime\prime}
be the solutions of (
1.1
) with drifts
b^{\prime},b^{\prime\prime}
and initial conditions
u^{\prime}_{0},u^{\prime\prime}_{0}
, respectively. Suppose that

\displaystyle u^{\prime}_{0}(x)\leqslant u^{\prime\prime}_{0}(x)\quad\text{for almost all $x\in{D}$};

\displaystyle b^{\prime}\preceq b^{\prime\prime}.

Then almost surely
u^{\prime}(t,x)\leqslant u^{\prime\prime}(t,x)
for all
t>0
,
x\in{D}
.

Our second applications of
2.10
shows that the skew stochastic
heat equation appears naturally as a scaling limit of certain SPDEs.

Let us introduce the space
\mathcal{C}_{\textrm{uc}}((0,T_{0}]\times{D})
of real continuous functions on
(0,T_{0}]\times{D}
equipped with the topology of uniform convergence over compact sets. It is well-known that this topology is induced by the metric

{d_{\textrm{uc}}}(f,h):=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}2^{-n}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in{D},|x|\leqslant n\\
t\in[\frac{1}{n},T_{0}]\end{subarray}}(|f(t,x)-h(t,x)|\wedge 1),\quad f,h\in\mathcal{C}_{\textrm{uc}}((0,T_{0}]\times{D})

(2.8)

and
\mathcal{C}_{\textrm{uc}}((0,T_{0}]\times{D})
is separable.

We define the Schwarz distributions
\zeta^{-1}
on
\mathbb{R}
by

\zeta^{-1}(\varphi):=\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}\int_{|x|>\varepsilon}\frac{\varphi(x)}{x}dx=\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\varphi(x)-\varphi(-x)}{x}\,dx

(2.9)

for each Schwarz function
\varphi
. Similarly, for
\alpha\in(-1,0)
we put

\zeta^{\alpha}_{+}(x):=x^{\alpha}\1(x>0)\quad\textrm{and}\quad\zeta^{\alpha}_{-}(x):=|x|^{\alpha}\1(x<0),

(2.10)

where
x\in\mathbb{R}
.

In the following result we consider the stochastic heat equation with
D=\mathbb{R}
.

Theorem 2.12
.

Let
\rho\in[1,3/2)
,
f:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
be a bounded continuous function. Let
u_{0}\in{\mathbf{B}}(\mathbb{R})
and for each
\lambda>0
let
u_{\lambda}
be the solution to

\partial_{t}u_{\lambda}(t,x)-\frac{1}{2}\partial^{2}_{xx}u_{\lambda}(t,x)=\lambda^{-\rho}f(u_{\lambda}(t,x))+\dot{W},\quad t\geqslant 0,\,\,x\in\mathbb{R},

(2.11)

with the initial condition
u_{\lambda}(0,\cdot)=u_{0}(\cdot)
.

1.

Assume that
\rho=1
and

\displaystyle\lim_{\lambda\to\infty}\int_{|x|>\lambda}\Big|f(x)-\frac{c}{x}\Big|dx=0\quad\textrm{and}\quad\lim_{\lambda\to\infty}\int_{|x|\leqslant\lambda}f(x)dx=c_{0}

(2.12)

for some constants
c,c_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
. Then the random field

\{\lambda^{-1/2}u_{\lambda}(\lambda^{2}t,\lambda x):(t,x)\in(0,1]\times\mathbb{R}\}

converges weakly in the space
\mathcal{C}_{\textrm{uc}}((0,1]\times\mathbb{R})
as
\lambda\to\infty
to the solution of the stochastic heat equation

\partial_{t}u(t,x)-\frac{1}{2}\partial^{2}_{xx}u(t,x)=(c\zeta^{-1}+c_{0}\delta_{0})(u(t,x))+\dot{W},\quad t\in[0,1],\,\,x\in\mathbb{R}

(2.13)

with the initial condition
u_{0}\equiv 0
.

2.

Assume that
\rho\in(1,3/2)
and

\lim_{x\to+\infty}f(x)x^{3-2\rho}=c_{+}\quad\textrm{and}\quad\lim_{x\to-\infty}f(x)|x|^{3-2\rho}=c_{-}

(2.14)

for some constants
c_{-},c_{+}\in\mathbb{R}
. Then the random field

\{\lambda^{-1/2}u_{\lambda}(\lambda^{2}t,\lambda x):(t,x)\in(0,1]\times\mathbb{R}\}

converges weakly in the space
\mathcal{C}_{\textrm{uc}}((0,1]\times\mathbb{R})
as
\lambda\to\infty
to the solution of the stochastic heat equation

\partial_{t}u(t,x)-\frac{1}{2}\partial^{2}_{xx}u(t,x)=(c_{-}\zeta^{2\rho-3}_{-}+c_{+}\zeta^{2\rho-3}_{+})(u(t,x))+\dot{W},\quad t\in[0,1],\,\,x\in\mathbb{R}

(2.15)

with the initial condition
u_{0}\equiv 0
.

Remark 2.13
.

It easy to see that if
f
is absolutely integrable on
\mathbb{R}
, then
c=0
,
c_{0}=\int_{\mathbb{R}}f(x)dx
, and one deduces a convergence to the skew stochastic heat equation from part (i) of the above result. In such case,
2.12
(i) is an analogue of
[
Ros75
]
(see also
[
LG84
, Corollary 3.3]
) for stochastic partial differential equations.

Remark 2.14
.

It is known that any homogeneous distribution on
\mathbb{R}
of order
\alpha\in(-1,0)
is a linear combination of
\zeta^{\alpha}_{+}
and
\zeta^{\alpha}_{-}
, and any homogeneous distribution on
\mathbb{R}
of order
-1
is a linear combination of
\delta_{0}
and
\zeta^{-1}
,
[
GS16
, Chapter I]
. Therefore,
2.12
shows that for any homogeneous distribution
h
of order
\alpha\in[-1,0)
, one can easily find a continuous function
f
, so that the corresponding scaling
limit of (
2.11
) converges to stochastic heat equation with
drift
h
.

2.2
Overview of the proofs of the main results

Before we proceed to the proofs of our main results, we would like to demonstrate our strategy on the following simple example, provide an overview of our arguments, and highlight the main challenges arising in the proofs.
We hope that this would help the reader to better understand our method and grasp the main ideas without having to dive into too many technical details

Thus, in the section we first consider the uniqueness problem for the equation (
1.1
), where the initial condition
u_{0}\equiv 0
, the drift
b
is a function (not a distribution) and
b\in\mathcal{C}^{\beta}=\mathcal{B}^{\beta}_{\infty}
with
\beta\in(0,1)
. Furthermore, we consider this equation on the time horizon
[0,\ell]
rather than
[0,T_{0}]
, where
\ell\in(0,1)
is small enough and to be chosen later.

As mentioned before, in this setting (
1.1
) is equivalent to
Eq(
0;b
)
. Assume the contrary and suppose that this equation has two solutions
u
and
v
.
We define

\psi_{t}:=u_{t}-V_{t};\,\,\varphi_{t}:=v_{t}-V_{t};\,\,z_{t}:=u_{t}-v_{t}=\psi_{t}-\varphi_{t},\quad t\in[0,\ell].

We note that
z(0)=0
and our goal is to prove that
z(t)=0
for all
t\in[0,\ell]
. We clearly have for any
t\in[0,\ell]
,
x\in{D}

\|z_{t}(x)\|_{L_{2}}=\Bigl\|\int_{0}^{t}\int_{D}p_{t-r}(x,y)\bigl(b(V_{r}(y)+\psi_{r}(y))-b(V_{r}(y)+\varphi_{r}(y))\bigr)dydr\Bigr\|_{{L_{2}}}.

(2.16)

A naive (and wrong) approach would be to then use directly the fact that
b\in\mathcal{C}^{\beta}
and to put the
\|\cdot\|_{{L_{2}}}
norm inside the integral. Then one would get

\|z_{t}(x)\|_{L_{2}}\leqslant\|b\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\beta}}\int_{0}^{t}\sup_{y\in{D}}\|z_{r}(y)\|^{\beta}_{{L_{2}}}dr

and hence

\sup_{t\in[0,\ell]}\sup_{x\in{D}}\|z_{t}(x)\|_{L_{2}}\leqslant\ell\|b\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\beta}}\sup_{t\in[0,\ell]}\sup_{x\in{D}}\|z_{t}(x)\|^{\beta}_{{L_{2}}}.

(2.17)

Since
\beta\in(0,1)
, it is obvious that neither of the above inequalities allows to conclude that
\sup_{y\in{D}}\|z_{t}(y)\|=0
. Instead, our aim is show that the following trade-off holds: one can have (
2.17
) with the factor
\|z_{t}(x)\|_{{L_{2}}}
in the power
1
and the price to pay is that factor
\ell
will be in a certain power smaller than
1
. However this will not obstruct the final conclusion.

To show this we are planning to work directly with the integral in the right-hand side of (
2.16
) and exploit the regularizing properties of the white noise. Recall that in the SDE setting it is known that

\Bigl\|\int_{0}^{t}(b(B_{r}^{H}+x_{1})-b(B_{r}^{H}+x_{2}))\,dr\Bigr\|_{{L_{2}}}\leqslant C\|b\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\beta}}t^{1+H(\beta-1)}|x_{1}-x_{2}|,\,\,t\geqslant 0,x_{1},x_{2}\in\mathbb{R},

(2.18)

where
B^{H}
denotes the fractional Brownian motion (fBM) with Hurst index
H\in(0,1)
and
\beta>1-1/(2H)
, see
[
Dav07b
, Proposition 2.1]
,
[
CG16
, Theorem 1.1]
. This is a trade-off we are aiming at. Unfortunately, Davie’s argument does not allow for an easy extension beyond the Brownian case;
note that, additionally, we would like to replace constants
x_{1}
,
x_{2}
in (
2.18
) by drifts (random fields)
\psi
,
\varphi
which depend on the spacial and time variables.

Therefore, we apply the stochastic sewing lemma (
4.1
) to conclude that for any

0\leqslant s\leqslant t\leqslant\ell
,
\tau\in[1/4,1]

\displaystyle\sup_{x\in{D}}\|z_{t}(x)-P_{t-s}z_{s}(x)\|_{{L_{2}}}

\displaystyle\quad=\sup_{x\in{D}}\Bigl\|\int_{s}^{t}\int_{D}p_{t-r}(x,y)\bigl(b(V_{r}(y)+\psi_{r}(y))-b(V_{r}(y)+\varphi_{r}(y))\bigr)dydr\Bigr\|_{{L_{2}}}

\displaystyle\quad\leqslant C\|b\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\beta}}\|z\|_{{\mathcal{C}^{0,0}L_{2}({[s,t]})}}(t-s)^{\frac{3}{4}+\frac{\beta}{4}}+C\|b\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\beta}}[z]_{{\mathcal{C}^{\tau,0}L_{2}({[s,t]})}}(t-s)^{\frac{3}{4}+\frac{\beta}{4}+\tau},

(2.19)

where we used the notation

\displaystyle\|z\|_{{\mathcal{C}^{0,0}L_{2}({[s,t]})}}:=\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}r\in[s,t]\\
x\in{D}\end{subarray}}\|z_{r}(x)\|_{{L_{2}}};

\displaystyle[z]_{{\mathcal{C}^{\tau,0}L_{2}({[s,t]})}}:=\sup_{s\leqslant s^{\prime}\leqslant t^{\prime}\leqslant t}\,\sup_{x\in{D}}\frac{\|z_{t^{\prime}}(x)-P_{t^{\prime}-s^{\prime}}z_{s^{\prime}}(x)\|_{{L_{m}}}}{|t^{\prime}-s^{\prime}|^{\tau}}.

Remark 2.15
.

The exponent
\frac{3}{4}+\frac{\beta}{4}
in (
2.2
) can be written as
H(\beta-1)+1
for
H=\frac{1}{4}
, which is the same as in (
2.18
). This is due to the local nondeterministic property of
V
in (
2.5
).
Note that fBM with
H=1/4
satisfies a very similar local nondeterministic property. This provides another connection of our results and the results in
[
CG16
]
concerning regularization by noise for fBM.

Now let us apply (
2.2
) with
\tau=3/4+\beta/4
, divide both sides of the inequality by
|t-s|^{3/4+\beta/4}
and take supremum over all
0\leqslant s\leqslant t\leqslant\ell
. We get

[z]_{{\mathcal{C}^{3/4+\beta/4,0}L_{2}({[0,\ell]})}}\leqslant C\|b\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\beta}}\|z\|_{{\mathcal{C}^{0,0}L_{2}({[0,\ell]})}}+C\|b\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\beta}}[z]_{{\mathcal{C}^{3/4+\beta/4,0}L_{2}({[0,\ell]})}}\ell^{\frac{3}{4}+\frac{\beta}{4}}.

(2.20)

Since the constant
C
does not depend on
\ell
, we can choose
\ell
small enough so that

C\|b\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\beta}}\ell^{\frac{3}{4}+\frac{\beta}{4}}\leqslant 1/2
. Substituting this back into (
2.20
), we get

[z]_{{\mathcal{C}^{3/4+\beta/4,0}L_{2}({[0,\ell]})}}\leqslant C\|b\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\beta}}\|z\|_{{\mathcal{C}^{0,0}L_{2}({[0,\ell]})}}.

Applying this bound to (
2.2
), setting there
s=0
, and taking there supremum over all
0\leqslant t\leqslant\ell
, we finally obtain

\|z\|_{{\mathcal{C}^{0,0}L_{2}({[0,\ell]})}}\leqslant C\|b\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\beta}}(1+\|b\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\beta}})\|z\|_{{\mathcal{C}^{0,0}L_{2}({[0,\ell]})}}\ell^{\frac{3}{4}+\frac{\beta}{4}}.

(2.21)

Provided that
\ell
is small enough, this yields
\|z\|_{{\mathcal{C}^{0,0}L_{2}({[0,\ell]})}}=0
, and thus
Eq(
0;b
)
has a unique strong solution.

Weak existences of solutions to (
1.1
) would follow from similar bounds (see
5.2
) and Prokhorov’s theorem. Finally, strong existence follows from weak existence and strong uniqueness by the Yamada–Watanabe principle (by the method of
[
GK96
]
).

While the uniqueness proof outlined above is quite short and “almost” rigorous, two major obstacles appears when one tries to extend this proof to cover distributional drifts
b\in\mathcal{C}^{\beta}
,
\beta<0
, and especially the case
b=\delta_{0}
.

First, for
\beta<0
the right-hand side of (
2.2
) contains the additional factor

\sup_{s\leqslant s^{\prime}\leqslant t^{\prime}\leqslant t}\,\sup_{x\in{D}}\esssup_{\omega\in\Omega}\frac{\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt[|\psi_{t^{\prime}}(x)-P_{t^{\prime}-s^{\prime}}\psi_{s^{\prime}}(x)|^{2}|\mathcal{F}_{s^{\prime}}]}{|t^{\prime}-s^{\prime}|^{1+\frac{\beta\wedge 0}{4}}}=:[\psi]^{2}_{{\mathcal{C}^{1+\frac{\beta\wedge 0}{4},0}L_{2,\infty}({[s,t]})}}.

(2.22)

When
b
was a bounded function and
\beta\geqslant 0
, it was obvious that

|\psi_{t^{\prime}}(x)-P_{t^{\prime}-s^{\prime}}\psi_{s^{\prime}}(x)|=\int_{s^{\prime}}^{t^{\prime}}\int_{D}p_{t^{\prime}-r}(x,y)b(V_{r}(y)+\psi_{r}(y))\,dydr\leqslant|t^{\prime}-s^{\prime}|\sup_{z\in\mathbb{R}}|b(z)|

and thus this extra factor was finite. Now when
b
is a distribution, the finiteness of this extra factor is not clear at all (note also the appearance of
\esssup
there).

The second obstacle is even more hindering. It turns out that bound (
2.2
) is valid only
for
\beta>-1
and thus is not applicable to the case where
b
is the Dirac delta function. This is similar to the fact that the corresponding bound for fBM with the Hurst parameter
1/4
, (
2.18
), is also known to be valid only for
\beta>-1
, see
[
CG16
, Theorem 1.1]
. While it is true that the Dirac delta function actually has better regularity and belongs to
\mathcal{B}^{-1+1/q}_{q}
for any
q\in[1,\infty]
, this does not help much. Indeed, one can show that bounds (
2.18
) and (
2.2
) hold for
b\in\mathcal{B}^{\beta}_{q}
with
\beta-1/q>-1
; however this still does not cover the delta function.

Let us explain now how we are overcoming these obstacles. A crucial role in our approach belongs to
Proposition

3.6
. It shows that if
u,v\in\mathcal{V}(3/4)
are two weak solutions to (
1.1
) adapted to the same filtration, and if for one of them expression (
2.22
) is finite, then these solutions coincide. To obtain this proposition we
combine the critical stochastic sewing lemma (
4.5
, extension of the stochastic sewing lemma from
[
Lê20
]
and
[
FHL21
, Lemma 2.9]
) with a very delicate analysis of the solution to (
1.1
). Bound (
2.2
) (which is not valid for the case
b=\delta_{0}
) is replaced by (
5.7
), see
5.7
. Note that we have to use a certain rough-path inspired expansion of the solution and bound its norm as well, see (
5.34
). Since new bound
(
5.7
) contains now some logarithmic terms, the final part of the uniqueness proof is less straightforward compared with (
2.21
), see
Section

5.2
. Our argument there is a stochastic analogue of Davie’s argument in
[
Dav07a
, Theorem 3.6]
.

Now we are ready to outline our strategy for establishing strong existence and uniqueness for equation (
1.1
).

Step 1.

We show that for any solution
u^{\eta;f}
to
Eq(
\eta;f
)
, where
\eta
is a bounded initial condition and
f
is a smooth function, the additional factor
[u^{\eta;f}-V]_{{\mathcal{C}^{1+\frac{\beta}{4},0}L_{2,\infty}({[0,\ell]})}}
from
(
2.22
) is finite and is bounded by a constant which depends only on the norm
\|f\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\beta}}
, see
Proposition

3.2
. This is done using regularization bounds from
5.2
.

Step 2
. At this step we fix two sequences of smooth functions

(b_{n}^{\prime})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}}
,
(b_{n}^{\prime\prime})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}}
converging to
b
in
\mathcal{B}^{\beta-}_{q}
and denote
by
u_{n}^{\prime}
,
u_{n}^{\prime\prime}
the solutions of
Eq(
u_{0};b_{n}^{\prime}
)
,
Eq(
u_{0};b_{n}^{\prime\prime}
)
, respectively.
Then, using again bounds from
5.2
, we are able to show that the sequence
(u_{n}^{\prime},u_{n}^{\prime\prime})
is tight. By Prokhorov’s theorem, this implies that it has a subsequence which converges weakly. We denote its limit by
(u^{\prime},u^{\prime\prime})
. This is done in
Proposition

3.3
.

Step 3
. Now we show that both
u^{\prime}
and
u^{\prime\prime}
solve (
1.1
), belong to
\mathcal{V}(3/4)
and the factor
[u^{\prime}-V]_{{\mathcal{C}^{1+\frac{\beta}{4},0}L_{2,\infty}({[0,\ell]})}}
is finite.
This is the content of
Propositions

3.4
and
3.5
.

Step 4
. Now we have two solutions
u^{\prime},u^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{V}(3/4)
for which the extra factor
from (
2.22
) is finite. Hence, by
Proposition

3.6
discussed above
u^{\prime}=u^{\prime\prime}
. This implies, thanks to a Yamada-Watanabe type result from
[
GK96
, Lemma 1.1]
, that
u^{\prime}
is actually a strong solution to (
1.1
), see the proof of
2.10
.

Step 5
. Now if
v\in\mathcal{V}(3/4)
is any other solution (for which the factor from
(
2.22
) is not necessary finite), it still coincides with the strong solution
u^{\prime}
constructed at the previous step. This is again due to
Proposition

3.6
, see
the proof of
2.6
(ii).

Step 6
. Finally we show that the extra condition
u\in\mathcal{V}(3/4)
is automatically satisfied for SPDEs with measure valued drift. This is done in
Proposition

3.8
using stochastic sewing lemma with random controls (
4.7
). This proves
2.8
.

Thus, we see that regularization estimates (
5.2
and
5.7
) play a very important role in our proofs. They are obtained using a flexible toolkit of stochastic sewing, which extends upon the original stochastic sewing from
[
Lê20
]
. For the convenience of the reader, all sewing results are stated separately in
Section

4
.

3
Proofs of the main results

In this section we prove the main results stated in
Section

2.1
. The technical parts, including the regularization estimates, are stated as propositions. The proofs of these propositions are postponed to the following sections.
First, we set up some necessary notation.

For
0\leqslant S<T
we denote by
\Delta_{S,T}
the simplex
\{(s,t):S\leqslant s\leqslant t\leqslant T\}
.
Let
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},(\mathcal{F}_{t})_{t\geqslant 0},\mathsf{P})
be a complete filtered probability space on which the white noise
W
is defined. We assume that the filtration
\mathcal{F}=(\mathcal{F}_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}
satisfies the usual condition and that
W
is
(\mathcal{F}_{t})
-white noise. We will write
\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s}
for the conditional expectation given
\mathcal{F}_{s}

\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s}[\cdot]:=\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt[\cdot|\mathcal{F}_{s}],\quad s\geqslant 0.

For a random process
Z\colon[0,T_{0}]\times{D}\times\Omega\to\mathbb{R}
we will denote by
(\mathcal{F}_{t}^{Z})
its natural filtration.

If
\mathscr{G}\subset\mathcal{F}
is a sub-
\sigma
-algebra, then we introduce the conditional quantity

\|\xi\|_{{L_{m}}|\mathscr{G}}:=\left(\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt[|\xi|^{m}|\mathscr{G}]\right)^{\frac{1}{m}},

(3.1)

which is a
\mathscr{G}
-measurable non-negative random variable.
It is evident that for
1\leqslant m\leqslant n\leqslant\infty
one has

\|\xi\|_{L_{m}}=\|\|\xi\|_{{L_{m}}|\mathscr{G}}\|_{{L_{m}}}\leqslant\|\|\xi\|_{{L_{m}}|\mathscr{G}}\|_{{L_{n}}}\leqslant\|\xi\|_{L_{n}}.

(3.2)

Let
0\leqslant S\leqslant T
. Let
\psi\colon[S,T]\times{D}\times\Omega\to\mathbb{R}
be a measurable function. For
\tau\in(0,1]
,
m,n\in[1,\infty]
define

\displaystyle[\psi]_{{\mathcal{C}^{\tau,0}L_{m}({[S,T]})}}:=\sup_{(s,t)\in\Delta_{S,T}}\sup_{x\in{D}}\frac{\|\psi_{t}(x)-P_{t-s}\psi_{s}(x)\|_{{L_{m}}}}{|t-s|^{\tau}};

\displaystyle[\psi]_{{\mathcal{C}^{\tau,0}L_{m,n}}([S,T])}:=\sup_{(s,t)\in\Delta_{S,T}}\sup_{x\in{D}}\frac{\|\|\psi_{t}(x)-P_{t-s}\psi_{s}(x)\|_{{L_{m}}|\mathcal{F}_{s}}\|_{L_{n}}}{|t-s|^{\tau}};

(3.3)

\displaystyle\|\psi\|_{{\mathcal{C}^{0,0}L_{m}({[S,T]})}}:=\sup_{t\in[S,T]}\sup_{x\in{D}}\|\psi_{t}(x)\|_{{L_{m}}}.

It follows from (
3.2
) that for
1\leqslant m\leqslant n\leqslant\infty

[\psi]_{{\mathcal{C}^{\tau,0}L_{m}({[S,T]})}}=[\psi]_{{\mathcal{C}^{\tau,0}L_{m,m}({[S,T]})}}\leqslant[\psi]_{{\mathcal{C}^{\tau,0}L_{m,n}({[S,T]})}}\leqslant[\psi]_{{\mathcal{C}^{\tau,0}L_{n}({[S,T]})}}.

(3.4)

Let
{\mathbf{B}L_{m}}
, where
m\geqslant 1
, be the space of all measurable functions
D\times\Omega\to\mathbb{R}
such that

\|f\|_{{\mathbf{B}L_{m}}}:=\sup_{x\in D}\|f(x)\|_{{L_{m}}}<\infty.

(3.5)

Before we begin the proofs of our main results, we would like to claim that in
2.6
and
2.10
it suffices to consider only the case
q\in[2,\infty]
,
\beta<0
. Indeed, recall the following embedding between Besov spaces (
[
BCD11
, Proposition 2.71]
)

\mathcal{B}^{\beta}_{q_{1}}\textrm{ is continuously embedded in }\mathcal{B}^{\beta-\frac{1}{q_{1}}+\frac{1}{q_{2}}}_{q_{2}}\textrm{ for every }1\leqslant q_{1}\leqslant q_{2}\leqslant\infty.

(3.6)

In
2.6
(ii), when
b\in\mathcal{B}^{\beta}_{q}
, with
q\in[1,2)
, we use embedding
\mathcal{B}^{\beta}_{q}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{B}^{\bar{\beta}}_{2}
where
\bar{\beta}:=\beta-\frac{1}{q}+\frac{1}{2}
. Note that
\bar{\beta}-\frac{1}{2}=\beta-\frac{1}{q}\geqslant-1
and
\bar{\beta}>-1
. This means that the results of
2.6
(ii) for
b
in
\mathcal{B}^{\beta}_{q}
with
q\in[1,2)
are consequences of those with larger integrability components
q
. Exactly the same argument is valid for
2.6
(i) and
2.10
. Hence, we assume without loss of generality hereafter that
q\in[2,\infty]
.
Similarly, thanks to embedding
\mathcal{B}^{\beta}_{q}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{B}^{-\beta^{\prime}}_{q}
for all
\beta,\beta^{\prime}>0
, we see that the statements of
2.6
and
2.10
for
b\in\mathcal{B}^{\beta}_{q}
with
\beta\geqslant 0
follows from the results of these theorems for some
\beta<0
. Hence, we can also assume without loss of generality that
\beta<0
. To summarize, we have the following

Assumption 3.1
.

From now on and till the end of this section we fix
\beta<0
,
q\in[2,\infty]
,
b\in\mathcal{B}^{\beta}_{q}
,
u_{0}\in{\mathbf{B}}(D)
. We assume that
\beta-1/q>-3/2
.

We begin with the proof of the existence of the solutions to (
1.1
). It consists of several steps.

Proposition 3.2

(A priori estimate)
.

Let
m\in[2,\infty)
,
f:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
be a bounded continuous function in
\mathcal{B}^{\beta}_{q}
,
\eta\in{\mathbf{B}}(D)
. Let
u^{\eta;f}
be the solution to
Eq(
\eta;f
)
. Then there exists a constant
C=C(\beta,q,m,T_{0})>0
independent from
\eta,f
such that

[u^{\eta;f}-V]_{{\mathcal{C}^{1+\frac{\beta}{4}-\frac{1}{4q},0}L_{m,\infty}({[0,T_{0}]})}}\leqslant C\|f\|_{\mathcal{B}^{\beta}_{q}}.

(3.7)

To formulate the next two statements we consider the space
\mathcal{C}_{\textrm{uc}}([0,T_{0}]\times{D})
of real continuous functions on
[0,T_{0}]\times{D}
equipped with the topology of uniform convergence over compact sets of
[0,T_{0}]\times{D}
. It is well-known that
\mathcal{C}_{\textrm{uc}}([0,T_{0}]\times{D})
is a Polish space and metrizable by the following metric, similar to (
2.8
),

{\rho_{\textrm{uc}}}(f,h):=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}2^{-n}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in{D},|x|\leqslant n\\
t\in[0,T_{0}]\end{subarray}}(|f(t,x)-h(t,x)|\wedge 1),\quad f,h\in\mathcal{C}_{\textrm{uc}}([0,T_{0}]\times{D}).

Proposition 3.3

(Tightness)
.

Let
(b_{n}^{\prime})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}}
,
(b_{n}^{\prime\prime})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}}
be two sequences of bounded continuous functions converging to
b
in
\mathcal{B}^{\beta-}_{q}
. Let
(u_{0,n}^{\prime})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}}
,
(u_{0,n}^{\prime\prime})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}}
be two sequences of functions from
{\mathbf{B}}(D)
which both converge to
u_{0}
uniformly on
{D}
.
Let
u_{n}^{\prime}
,
u_{n}^{\prime\prime}
be the solutions of
Eq(
u_{0,n}^{\prime};b_{n}^{\prime}
)
,
Eq(
u_{0,n}^{\prime\prime};b_{n}^{\prime\prime}
)
, respectively. Put

\widetilde{u}_{n}^{\prime}(t,x):=u_{n}^{\prime}(t,x)-P_{t}u_{0,n}^{\prime}(x),\,\,\widetilde{u}_{n}^{\prime\prime}(t,x):=u_{n}^{\prime\prime}(t,x)-P_{t}u_{0,n}^{\prime\prime}(x),\quad t\in[0,T_{0}],\,\,x\in D.

Then there exists a subsequence
(n_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}}
such that
(\widetilde{u}_{n_{k}}^{\prime},\widetilde{u}_{n_{k}}^{\prime\prime},V)_{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}}
converges weakly in the space
[\mathcal{C}_{\textrm{uc}}([0,T_{0}]\times{D})]^{3}
.

Proposition 3.4

(Stability)
.

Let
(b^{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}}
be a sequence of bounded continuous functions converging to
b
in
\mathcal{B}^{\beta-}_{q}
. Let
(u_{0}^{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}}
be a sequence of functions from
{\mathbf{B}}({D})
converging to
u_{0}
uniformly on
{D}
. Let
V^{n}
be a random element having the same law as
V
.
Assume that
u^{n}
is a strong solution of
Eq(
u^{n}_{0};b^{n}
)
with
V^{n}
in place of
V
. Let

\widetilde{u}^{n}(t,x):=u^{n}(t,x)-P_{t}u_{0}^{n}(x),\quad t\in[0,T_{0}],\,\,x\in D.

Suppose that there exist measurable functions
\widetilde{u},V\colon[0,T_{0}]\times{D}\times\Omega\to\mathbb{R}
such that the sequence
(\widetilde{u}^{n},V^{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}}
converges to
(\widetilde{u},V)
in
[\mathcal{C}_{\textrm{uc}}([0,T_{0}]\times{D})]^{2}
in probability as
n\to\infty
.
Then the function

u(t,x):=\widetilde{u}(t,x)+P_{t}u_{0}(x),\quad t\in[0,T_{0}],\,\,x\in D

is a solution to (
1.1
) with the initial condition
u_{0}
and for any
m\in[2,\infty)
there exists
C=C(\beta,q,m,T_{0})>0
such that

[u-V]_{{\mathcal{C}^{1+\frac{\beta}{4}-\frac{1}{4q},0}L_{m,\infty}({[0,T_{0}]})}}\leqslant C\sup_{n\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}}\|b^{n}\|_{\mathcal{B}^{\beta}_{q}}<\infty.

(3.8)

The proofs of
Propositions

3.2
,
3.3
and
3.4
are presented in
Section

5.1
.

Combining the above propositions we obtain the following corollary, which immediately implies
2.6
(i). This corollary will be also important to show the existence of strong solutions to (
1.1
).

Corollary 3.5
.

In the setting of
Proposition

3.3
the following holds. There exists a filtered probability space
(\widehat{\Omega},\widehat{\mathcal{F}},(\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{t})_{t\in[0,T_{0}]},\widehat{P})
, an
(\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{t})
-white noise
\widehat{W}
defined on this space, measurable functions
v^{\prime},v^{\prime\prime}\colon[0,T_{0}]\times{D}\times\widehat{\Omega}\to\mathbb{R}
such that

1.

both
v^{\prime}
and
v^{\prime\prime}
are adapted to the filtration
(\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{t})
and are weak solutions to (
1.1
) with the initial condition
u_{0}
;

2.

there exists a subsequence
(n_{k})
such that
(\widetilde{u}_{n_{k}}^{\prime},\widetilde{u}_{n_{k}}^{\prime\prime})_{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}}
converges weakly to
(\widetilde{v}^{\prime},\widetilde{v}^{\prime\prime})
in the space
[\mathcal{C}_{\textrm{uc}}([0,T_{0}]\times{D})]^{2}
as
k\to\infty
, where

\widetilde{v}^{\prime}(t,x):=v^{\prime}(t,x)-P_{t}u_{0}(x),\,\,\widetilde{v}^{\prime\prime}(t,x):=v^{\prime\prime}(t,x)-P_{t}u_{0}(x),\quad t\in[0,T_{0}],\,\,x\in D.

(3.9)

3.

for
\widehat{V}
defined as in (
2.4
) with
\widehat{W}
in place of
W
the following holds:

[v^{\prime}-\widehat{V}]_{{\mathcal{C}^{1+\frac{\beta}{4}-\frac{1}{4q},0}L_{m,\infty}({[0,T_{0}]})}}+[v^{\prime\prime}-\widehat{V}]_{{\mathcal{C}^{1+\frac{\beta}{4}-\frac{1}{4q},0}L_{m,\infty}({[0,T_{0}]})}}<\infty.

(3.10)

Proof.

By
Proposition

3.3
, there exists a subsequence
(n_{k})
such that
(\widetilde{u}_{n_{k}}^{\prime},\widetilde{u}_{n_{k}}^{\prime\prime},V)_{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}}
converges weakly in the space
[\mathcal{C}_{\textrm{uc}}([0,T_{0}]\times{D})]^{3}
. By passing to this subsequence, to simplify the notation, we may assume without loss of generality that
(\widetilde{u}_{n}^{\prime},\widetilde{u}_{n}^{\prime\prime},V)
converges weakly. Since this space is Polish, we can apply the Skorohod representation theorem
[
Bil99
, Theorem 6.7]
and deduce that
there exists a sequence of random elements
(\widetilde{v}^{\prime}_{n},\widetilde{v}^{\prime\prime}_{n},\widehat{V}^{n})
defined on a common probability space
(\widehat{\Omega},\widehat{\mathcal{F}},\widehat{P})
and a random element
(\widetilde{v}^{\prime},\widetilde{v}^{\prime\prime},\widehat{V})
such that

\Law(\widetilde{v}^{\prime}_{n},\widetilde{v}^{\prime\prime}_{n},\widehat{V}^{n})=\Law(\widetilde{u}^{\prime}_{n},\widetilde{u}^{\prime\prime}_{n},V)

(3.11)

and
(\widetilde{v}^{\prime}_{n},\widetilde{v}^{\prime\prime}_{n},\widehat{V}^{n})
converges to
(\widetilde{v}^{\prime},\widetilde{v}^{\prime\prime},\widehat{V})
a.s. in space
[\mathcal{C}_{\textrm{uc}}([0,T_{0}]\times{D})]^{3}
.

Define for
t\in[0,T_{0}],\,\,x\in D

\displaystyle v_{n}^{\prime}(t,x):=\widetilde{v}_{n}^{\prime}(t,x)+P_{t}u^{\prime}_{0,n}(x),\,\,\,v_{n}^{\prime\prime}(t,x):=\widetilde{v}_{n}^{\prime\prime}(t,x)+P_{t}u^{\prime\prime}_{0,n}(x),

\displaystyle v^{\prime}(t,x):=\widetilde{v}^{\prime}(t,x)+P_{t}u^{\prime}_{0}(x),\,\,\,v^{\prime\prime}(t,x):=\widetilde{v}^{\prime\prime}(t,x)+P_{t}u^{\prime\prime}_{0}(x).

Since
u_{n}^{\prime}
satisfies
Eq(
u^{\prime}_{0,n};b_{n}^{\prime}
)
, by (
3.11
) we have that
v_{n}^{\prime}
satisfies
Eq(
u^{\prime}_{0,n};b_{n}^{\prime}
)
with
\widehat{V}^{n}
in place of
V
.

Since
u_{n}^{\prime}
is the strong solution to
Eq(
u^{\prime}_{0,n};b_{n}^{\prime}
)
, the random variable
u_{n}^{\prime}(t,x)
is
\mathcal{F}_{t}^{W}
-measurable, where
t\in(0,T_{0}]
,
x\in{D}
. By
Lemma

B.2
,
\mathcal{F}_{t}^{W}=\mathcal{F}_{t}^{V}
. This and (
3.11
) implies that
v_{n}^{\prime}(t,x)
is
\mathcal{F}_{t}^{\widehat{V}^{n}}
-measurable. By
Lemma

B.2
, there exists a white noise
\widehat{W}^{n}
such that (
2.4
) holds for
\widehat{W}^{n}
in place of
W
and
\widehat{V}^{n}
in place of
\widehat{V}
and
\mathcal{F}_{t}^{\widehat{V}^{n}}=\mathcal{F}_{t}^{\widehat{W}^{n}}
.
Thus
v_{n}^{\prime}(t,x)
is
\mathcal{F}_{t}^{\widehat{W}^{n}}
-measurable. Identity (
3.11
) implies now that
v_{n}^{\prime}
is a strong solution to
Eq(
u^{\prime}_{0,n};b_{n}^{\prime}
)
with
\widehat{V}^{n}
in place of
V
. Similarly,
v_{n}^{\prime\prime}
is a strong solution to
Eq(
u^{\prime\prime}_{0,n};b_{n}^{\prime\prime}
)
with
\widehat{V}^{n}
in place of
V
.

We see now that all the conditions of
Proposition

3.4
are satisfied. Applying this result, we see that
v^{\prime}
and
v^{\prime\prime}
are solutions to (
1.1
) in the sense of
2.3
with
\widehat{V}
in place of
V
.

Define now
\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{t}:=\sigma(v^{\prime}_{r}(x),\,v^{\prime\prime}_{r}(x),\,\widehat{V}_{r}(x),x\in{D},r\in[0,t])
.
Clearly,
v^{\prime}
and
v^{\prime\prime}
are
\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{t}
measurable.

It follows immediately from the definition of the white noise that for any
(s,t)\in\Delta_{0,T_{0}}
,
\varphi\in\mathcal{C}_{c}^{\infty}
,
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
the random variable

\int_{D}\int_{s}^{t}\int_{D}\varphi(x)p_{t-r}(x,y)\widehat{W}^{n}(dy,dr)dx=\int_{{D}}\varphi(x)(\widehat{V}^{n}_{t}(x)-P_{t-s}\widehat{V}^{n}_{s}(x))\,dx

is independent of
\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{s}^{\widehat{W}^{n}}=\sigma(v_{n}^{\prime}(r,x),\,v_{n}^{\prime\prime}(r,x),\,\widehat{V}^{n}_{r}(x),x\in{D},r\in[0,s])
. Therefore,
Proposition

B.1
implies that the random variable
\int_{{D}}\varphi(x)(\widehat{V}_{t}(x)-P_{t-s}\widehat{V}_{s}(x))\,dx
is independent of
\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{s}
. Thus, by
Lemma

B.2
, there exists an
(\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{t})
-white noise
\widehat{W}
such that (
2.4
) holds for
\widehat{W}
in place of
W
and
\widehat{V}
in place of
\widehat{V}
.
Hence
v^{\prime}
and
v^{\prime\prime}
are weak solutions to (
1.1
) and they are adapted to the same filtration
(\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{t})
.

Finally, it remains to note that (
3.10
) follows now from (
3.8
).
∎

Proof of
2.6
(i).

Let
(b^{n})
be a sequence of smooth functions converging to
b
in
\mathcal{B}^{\beta-}_{p}
. Applying
3.5
with
b_{n}^{\prime}=b_{n}^{\prime\prime}=b^{n}
and
u_{0,n}^{\prime}=u_{0,n}^{\prime\prime}=u_{0}
we obtain existence of a weak solution
v^{\prime}
. By (
3.10
),

[v^{\prime}-\widehat{V}]_{{\mathcal{C}^{1+\frac{\beta}{4}-\frac{1}{4q},0}L_{m}({[0,T_{0}]})}}\leqslant[v^{\prime}-\widehat{V}]_{{\mathcal{C}^{1+\frac{\beta}{4}-\frac{1}{4q},0}L_{m,\infty}({[0,T_{0}]})}}<\infty,

where the first inequality follows from (
3.2
). Hence
v^{\prime}\in\mathcal{V}(1+\frac{\beta}{4}-\frac{1}{4q})
.
∎

Now we move on to the proofs of strong existence and uniqueness of solutions to (
1.1
).

Proposition 3.6

(Uniqueness)
.

Suppose additionally that
\beta\geqslant-1+1/q
,
\beta>-1
.
Let
(u_{t})_{t\in[0,T_{0}]}
be a solution of SPDE (
1.1
) starting from the initial condition
u_{0}
. Let
(v_{t})_{t\in[0,T_{0}]}
be a measurable process
(0,T_{0}]\times{D}\times\Omega\to\mathbb{R}
, which satisfies conditions (1), (3) of
2.3
and condition
(2′)
of
Remark

2.7
.

Suppose that
u,v
are adapted to the filtration
(\mathcal{F}_{t})_{t\in[0,T_{0}]}
and belong to the class
\mathcal{V}(3/4)
. Assume further that for some
m\geqslant 2

[u-V]_{{\mathcal{C}^{\frac{3}{4},0}L_{m,\infty}({[0,T_{0}]})}}<\infty.

(3.12)

Then
u=v
a.s.

The proof of
Proposition

3.6
is given in
Section

5.2
.

The proof of strong existence uses the following statement from
[
GK96
]
. For the convenience of the reader we provide it here.

Proposition 3.7

(
[
GK96
, Lemma 1.1]
)
.

Let
(Z_{n})
be a sequence of random elements in a Polish space
(E,\rho)
equipped with the Borel
\sigma
-algebra. Assume that for every pair of subsequences

(Z_{l_{k}})
and
(Z_{m_{k}})
there exists a further sub-subsequence
(Z_{l_{k_{r}}},Z_{m_{k_{r}}})
which converges weakly in the space
E\times E
to a random element
w=(w^{1},w^{2})
such that
w^{1}=w^{2}
a.s.

Then there exists an
E
-valued random element
Z
such that
(Z_{n})
converges in probability
to
Z
.

Proof of
2.10
.

We will use
Proposition

3.7
. Fix a sequence
(b_{n})
of bounded continuous functions converging to
b
in
\mathcal{B}^{\beta-}_{q}
and a sequence
(u_{0,n})
of functions from
{\mathbf{B}}({D})
converging to
u_{0}
. Let
u_{n}
be the strong solution to
Eq(
u_{0,n};b_{n}
)
.
Define

\widetilde{u}_{n}(t,x):=u_{n}(t,x)-P_{t}u_{0,n}(x),\quad t\in[0,T_{0}],\,\,x\in D.

Let
(b^{\prime}_{n},\widetilde{u}^{\prime}_{n})
and
(b^{\prime\prime}_{n},\widetilde{u}^{\prime\prime}_{n})
be two arbitrary subsequences of
(b_{n},\widetilde{u}_{n})
. We apply
3.5
. It follows that there exists a filtered probability space
(\widehat{\Omega},\widehat{\mathcal{F}},(\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{t})_{t\in[0,T_{0}]},\widehat{P})
, an
(\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{t})
-white noise
\widehat{W}
defined on this space, and a pair of weak solutions
(v^{\prime},v^{\prime\prime})
to (
1.1
) adapted to the filtration
(\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{t})
. We see also that there exists a subsequence
(n_{k})
such that

(\widetilde{u}^{\prime}_{n_{k}},\widetilde{u}^{\prime\prime}_{n_{k}})
converges to
(\widetilde{v}^{\prime},\widetilde{v}^{\prime\prime})
weakly in the space
[\mathcal{C}_{\textrm{uc}}([0,T_{0}]\times{D})]^{2}
as
k\to\infty
, where
(\widetilde{v}^{\prime},\widetilde{v}^{\prime\prime})
are defined in (
3.9
).
We note that (
3.10
) together with (
3.2
) implies that for any
m\geqslant 2

[v^{\prime}-\widehat{V}]_{{\mathcal{C}^{\frac{3}{4},0}L_{m}({[0,T_{0}]})}}\leqslant[v^{\prime}-\widehat{V}]_{{\mathcal{C}^{\frac{3}{4},0}L_{m,\infty}({[0,T_{0}]})}}<\infty,

where we used the fact that
1+\beta/4-1/(4q)\geqslant 3/4
. Thus the pair
(v^{\prime},\widehat{V})
satisfies (
3.12
) and
v^{\prime}
belong to the class
\mathcal{V}(3/4)
. Similarly,
v^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{V}(3/4)
. Thus, we see that all the assumptions of
Proposition

3.6
are satisfied and we can conclude that
v^{\prime}=v^{\prime\prime}
a.s. By definition, this implies that
\widetilde{v}^{\prime}=\widetilde{v}^{\prime\prime}
a.s.

Thus, all the conditions of
Proposition

3.7
are met. Hence there exists a

\mathcal{C}_{\textrm{uc}}([0,T_{0}]\times{D})
-valued random element
\widetilde{u}
such that
\widetilde{u}_{n}
converges to
\widetilde{u}
in probability as
n\to\infty
. Set now

u(t,x):=\widetilde{u}(t,x)+P_{t}u_{0}(x),\quad t\in[0,T_{0}],\,\,x\in D.

Applying
Proposition

3.4
, we see that
u
is a solution to (
1.1
) with the initial condition
u_{0}
. Since
\widetilde{u}_{n}(t,x)
, where
t\in[0,T_{0}]
,
x\in{D}
, is

\mathcal{F}_{t}^{W}
-measurable, we see that
\widetilde{u}(t,x)
and, hence
u(t,x)

are
\mathcal{F}_{t}^{W}
-measurable. Thus,
u
is a strong solution to (
1.1
).

From the convergence of probability of
\widetilde{u}_{n}
to
\widetilde{u}
, we get that for any
N>0

\sup_{t\in[0,T_{0}]}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in{D}\\
|x|\leqslant N\end{subarray}}|\widetilde{u}^{n}_{t}(x)-\widetilde{u}_{t}(x)|\to 0\quad\text{in probability as $n\to\infty$}.

(3.13)

Further, by the assumptions of the theorem

\sup_{t\in[0,T_{0}]}\sup_{x\in{D}}|P_{t}u^{n}_{0}(x)-P_{t}u_{0}(x)|\leqslant\sup_{x\in{D}}|u^{n}_{0}(x)-u_{0}(x)|\to 0\quad\text{as $n\to\infty$},

(3.14)

where we used the fact that
|P_{t}f(x)|\leqslant\sup_{y}|f(y)|
for any bounded function
f
. Combining (
3.13
) and (
3.14
), we obtain (
2.6
).

Finally, part (3) of the theorem follows from (
3.8
) and the fact that
1+\frac{\beta}{4}-\frac{1}{4q}\geqslant\frac{3}{4}
.
∎

Proof of
2.6
(ii).

By
2.10
, there exists a strong solution
u
to (
1.1
) satisfying (
3.12
). If
v
is another strong solution to (
1.1
) in the class
\mathcal{V}(3/4)
, then, by
Proposition

3.6

u=v
. This shows strong uniqueness of solutions to (
1.1
).
∎

Proposition 3.8
.

Suppose that
b
is a non-negative finite measure, then every solution of (
1.1
) belongs to the class
\mathcal{V}(3/4)
.

The proof of
Proposition

3.8
is given in
Section

5.3
.

Proof of
2.8
.

Let
u_{0}
be a bounded measurable function.
Since measures belong to
\mathcal{B}^{0}_{1}
,
2.6
yields existence and uniqueness of a strong solution
u
to (
1.1
) in
\mathcal{V}(3/4)
starting from
u_{0}
.
On the other hand, by
Proposition

3.8
, every solution to (
1.1
) belongs to
\mathcal{V}(3/4)
and thus has to coincide with
u
, thus completing the proof.
∎

Proof of
Corollary

2.11
.

The proof uses an idea similar to
[
GP93b
, Proof of Theorem 2.4]
.
For
n\in\mathbb{N}
, put
b_{n}^{\prime}:=G_{1/n}b^{\prime}
,
b_{n}^{\prime\prime}:=G_{1/n}b^{\prime\prime}
. By
Lemma

A.3
,
b_{n}^{\prime}
and
b_{n}^{\prime\prime}
are smooth and bounded. Let
u_{n}^{\prime}
be the strong solution to
Eq(
u_{0}^{\prime};b_{n}^{\prime}
)
and let
u_{n}^{\prime\prime}
be the strong solution to
Eq(
u_{0}^{\prime\prime};b_{n}^{\prime\prime}
)
. Note that
b^{\prime}\preceq b^{\prime\prime}
implies
b_{n}^{\prime}(x)\leqslant b_{n}^{\prime\prime}(x)
for any
x\in\mathbb{R}
, thanks to the definition of the partial order. Then, using again that
b_{n}^{\prime}
and
b_{n}^{\prime\prime}
are smooth and bounded, the standard comparison principle (see, e.g.,
[
GP93b
, Theorem 2.4]
,
[
BP90
, Lemma 3.3]
) yields

u_{n}^{\prime}(t,x)\leqslant u_{n}^{\prime\prime}(t,x),\quad t>0,\,x\in{D}.

(3.15)

By
Lemma

A.3
,
b_{n}^{\prime}\to b^{\prime}
,
b_{n}^{\prime\prime}\to b^{\prime\prime}
in
\mathcal{B}_{q}^{\beta-}
as
n\to\infty
. Therefore, by passing to the limit as
n\to\infty
in (
3.15
), we get for any fixed
t>0,\,x\in{D}
by
2.10
.

u^{\prime}(t,x)\leqslant u^{\prime\prime}(t,x),\,\,\text{a.s}.

Since
u^{\prime}
and
u^{\prime\prime}
are continuous, this implies that a.s.
u^{\prime}(t,x)\leqslant u^{\prime\prime}(t,x)
for all
t>0
,
x\in{D}
.
∎

Proof of
2.12
.

Define
f_{\lambda}(x)=\lambda^{3/2-\rho}f(\lambda^{1/2}x)
,
\bar{u}_{\lambda}(t,x)=\lambda^{-1/2}u_{\lambda}(\lambda^{2}t,\lambda x)
and
V_{\lambda}(t,x)=\lambda^{-1/2}V(\lambda^{2}t,\lambda x)
. By a change of variables, we have

\displaystyle\bar{u}_{\lambda}(t,x)=\lambda^{-1/2}\int_{\mathbb{R}}p_{t}(x-y)u_{0}(\lambda y)dy+V_{\lambda}(t,x)+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}}p_{t-r}(x-y)f_{\lambda}(\bar{u}_{\lambda}(r,y))drdy.

Note that the random fields
V_{\lambda}
and
V
have the same probability law. Hence,
\bar{u}_{\lambda}
is a weak solution to (
1.1
) with
f_{\lambda}
in place of
b
and
\bar{u}_{\lambda}(0,x)=\lambda^{-1/2}u_{0}(\lambda x)
.
It is straightforward to see that
\bar{u}_{\lambda}(0,\cdot)
converges to
0
uniformly on
\mathbb{R}
. If
\rho=1
, then by
Lemma

A.5
,
f_{\lambda}
converges to
c\zeta^{-1}+c_{0}\delta_{0}
in
\mathcal{B}^{(-1+1/p)_{-}}_{p}
as
\lambda\to\infty
for any
p\in(1,\infty)
. If
\rho\in(1,3/2)
, then by
Lemma

A.6
,
f_{\lambda}
converges to
c_{-}\zeta^{2\rho-3}_{-}+c_{+}\zeta^{2\rho-3}_{+}
in
\mathcal{B}^{(2\rho-3)_{-}}_{\infty}
as
\lambda\to\infty
.

Applying
2.10
, we see that if
\rho=1
, then the process
\bar{u}_{\lambda}
converges weakly in the space
\mathcal{C}_{\textrm{uc}}((0,1]\times\mathbb{R})
as
\lambda\to\infty
to the solution of (
2.13
). Similarly, if
\rho\in(1,3/2)
the same theorem implies that the process
\bar{u}_{\lambda}
converges weakly in the space
\mathcal{C}_{\textrm{uc}}((0,1]\times\mathbb{R})
as
\lambda\to\infty
to the solution of (
2.15
).
∎

4
Stochastic sewing lemmas

We present three extensions of the stochastic sewing lemma introduced earlier in
[
Lê20
]
. More precisely, we incorporate singularities, critical exponents and random controls in the stochastic sewing lemma.
In addition, we also provide estimates in some conditional moment norms, inspired by the stochastic sewing in
[
FHL21
]
.
As we will see in later sections, singularities allow for improvements of regularities and broaden the scope of applications of the stochastic sewing techniques (see for instance
6.1
and
6.2
in
Section

6
). The result with random controls (
4.7
below) is used in
Proposition

3.8
to obtain a priori estimates for solutions to
Eq.

1.1
when the drift
b
is a measure. The stochastic sewing result for critical exponent is used to prove
Proposition

3.6
, that is strong uniqueness for
Eq.

1.1
when
\beta-1/q=-1
. Finally, the estimates in conditional moment norms are also used in
Proposition

3.2
which is later used in
Proposition

3.6
to prevent a loss of integrability.
We believe that these results complement
[
Lê20
, Theorem 2.1]
,
[
FHL21
, Theorem 2.7]
and form a toolkit which is also of independent interest and can be useful for other purposes.

4.1
Statements of stochastic sewing lemmas

Till the end of this section we fix a time horizon
T\in(0,\infty)
and a filtered probability space
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},(\mathcal{F}_{t})_{t\in[0,T]},\mathsf{P})
. Recall that for
0\leqslant S<T
we denoted by
\Delta_{S,T}
the simplex
\{(s,t)\in[S,T]^{2}:s\leqslant t\}
. The mesh size of a partition
\Pi
of an interval will be denoted by
|\Pi|
.
Let
A:\Delta_{S,T}\to{L_{m}}
be such that
A_{s,t}
is
\mathcal{F}_{t}
-measurable for every
(s,t)\in\Delta_{S,T}
. For every triplet of times
(s,u,t)
such that
S\leqslant s\leqslant u\leqslant t\leqslant T
, we denote

\delta A_{s,u,t}:=A_{s,t}-A_{s,u}-A_{u,t}.

For each
\alpha,\beta\in[0,1)
,
(s,t)\in\Delta_{S,T}
define the function

\eta_{S,T}^{(\alpha,\beta)}(s,t):=\int_{s}^{t}(r-S)^{-\alpha}(T-r)^{-\beta}dr.

It is immediate that

\eta_{S,T}^{(\alpha,\beta)}(s,t)\leqslant C(\alpha,\beta)(t-s)^{1-\alpha-\beta}.

(4.1)

Recall the notation
\|\cdot\|_{L_{m}|\mathcal{F}_{t}}
introduced in (
3.1
).

Theorem 4.1

(Stochastic sewing lemma)
.

Let
m\in[2,\infty)
and
n\in[m,\infty]
be fixed.
Assume that there exist constants
\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2}\geqslant 0
,
\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2}>0
,
\alpha_{1},\beta_{1}\in[0,1)
and
\alpha_{2},\beta_{2}\in[0,\frac{1}{2})
such that the following conditions hold for every
(s,t)\in\Delta_{S,T}
and
u:=(s+t)/2

\displaystyle\|\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s}[\delta A_{s,u,t}]\|_{L_{n}}\leqslant\Gamma_{1}(u-S)^{-\alpha_{1}}(T-u)^{-\beta_{1}}|t-s|^{1+\varepsilon_{1}},

(4.2)

\displaystyle\|\|\delta A_{s,u,t}\|_{L_{m}|\mathcal{F}_{S}}\|_{L_{n}}\leqslant\Gamma_{2}(u-S)^{-\alpha_{2}}(T-u)^{-\beta_{2}}|t-s|^{\frac{1}{2}+\varepsilon_{2}}.

(4.3)

Further, suppose that there exists a process
\mathcal{A}=\{\mathcal{A}_{t}:t\in[S,T]\}
such that for any
t\in[S,T]
and any sequence of partitions
\Pi_{N}:=\{S=t^{N}_{0},...,t^{N}_{k(N)}=t\}
of
[S,t]
such that
\lim_{N\to\infty}|\Pi_{N}|\to 0
one has

\mathcal{A}_{t}=\lim_{N\to\infty}\sum_{i=0}^{k(N)-1}A_{t^{N}_{i},t^{N}_{i+1}}\,\,\text{in probability.}

(4.4)

Then there exists a constant
C=C(\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2},m)
independent of
S,T
such that
for every
(s,t)\in\Delta_{S,T}
we have

\|\|\mathcal{A}_{t}-\mathcal{A}_{s}-A_{s,t}\|_{L_{m}|\mathcal{F}_{S}}\|_{L_{n}}\leqslant C\Gamma_{2}\left(\eta_{S,T}^{(2\alpha_{2},2\beta_{2})}(s,t)\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}|t-s|^{\varepsilon_{2}}\\
+C\Gamma_{1}\eta_{S,T}^{(\alpha_{1},\beta_{1})}(s,t)|t-s|^{\varepsilon_{1}}

(4.5)

and

\|\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{S}[\mathcal{A}_{t}-\mathcal{A}_{s}-A_{s,t}]\,\|_{L_{n}}\leqslant C\Gamma_{1}\eta_{S,T}^{(\alpha_{1},\beta_{1})}(s,t)|t-s|^{\varepsilon_{1}}.

(4.6)

Remark 4.2
.

When
n=m
and
\alpha_{i}=\beta_{i}=0
,
i=1,2
, the estimates (
4.5
) and (
4.6
) coincide with those in
[
Lê20
, Theorem 2.1]
. Using the arguments in the aforementioned paper, one can obtain from (
4.2
) and (
4.3
) the existence the the process
\mathcal{A}
satisfying (
4.4
). However, by imposing condition (
4.4
), our presentation on the applications of
4.1
in later sections is simplified.

Remark 4.3
.

In view of (
3.2
), condition (
4.3
) follows from the following simpler condition. There exist constants
\Gamma_{2}\geqslant 0
,
\varepsilon_{2}>0
,
\alpha_{2},\beta_{2}\in[0,\frac{1}{2})
such that for every
S\leqslant s\leqslant t\leqslant T
,
u:=(s+t)/2
,
x\in D
one has

\|\delta A_{s,u,t}\|_{L_{n}}\leqslant\Gamma_{2}(u-S)^{-\alpha_{2}}(T-u)^{-\beta_{2}}|t-s|^{\frac{1}{2}+\varepsilon_{2}}.

(4.7)

Sometimes, it might be useful to apply the following modification of stochastic sewing lemma.

Theorem 4.4
.

Suppose that all the conditions of
4.1
are satisfied apart from (
4.4
), which is replaced by the following:
there exists a process
\mathcal{A}=\{\mathcal{A}_{t}:t\in[S,T]\}
such that for any
(s,t)\in\Delta_{S,T}
and any sequence of partitions
\Pi_{N}:=\{s=t^{N}_{0},...,t^{N}_{k(N)}=t\}
of
[s,t]
such that
\lim_{N\to\infty}|\Pi_{N}|\to 0
one has

|\mathcal{A}_{t}-\mathcal{A}_{s}|\leqslant\liminf_{N\to\infty}\Bigl|\sum_{i=0}^{k(N)-1}A_{t^{N}_{i},t^{N}_{i+1}}\Bigr|\quad\text{a.s.}

(4.8)

Then there exists a constant
C=C(\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2},m)
independent of
S,T
such that
for every
(s,t)\in\Delta_{S,T}
we have

\|\|\mathcal{A}_{t}-\mathcal{A}_{s}\|_{L_{m}|\mathcal{F}_{S}}\|_{L_{n}}\leqslant\|\|A_{s,t}\|_{L_{m}|\mathcal{F}_{S}}\|_{L_{n}}+C\Gamma_{2}\left(\eta_{S,T}^{(2\alpha_{2},2\beta_{2})}(s,t)\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}|t-s|^{\varepsilon_{2}}\\
+C\Gamma_{1}\eta_{S,T}^{(\alpha_{1},\beta_{1})}(s,t)|t-s|^{\varepsilon_{1}}

(4.9)

and

\|\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{S}[\mathcal{A}_{t}-\mathcal{A}_{s}]\,\|_{L_{n}}\leqslant\|\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{S}A_{s,t}\,\|_{L_{n}}+C\Gamma_{1}\eta_{S,T}^{(\alpha_{1},\beta_{1})}(s,t)|t-s|^{\varepsilon_{1}}.

(4.10)

The next result, which is used later in the proof of
Proposition

3.6
, is inspired by the stochastic Davie–Grönwall lemma from
[
FHL21
]
.

Theorem 4.5

(Stochastic sewing with critical exponent)
.

Let
m\in[2,\infty)
.
Assume that all the conditions of
4.1
are satisfied with the choice
n=m
,
\alpha_{1}=\beta_{1}=\alpha_{2}=\beta_{2}=0
.
Suppose additionally that there exist constants
\Gamma_{3}>0
,
\Gamma_{4}\geqslant 0
,
\varepsilon_{4}>0
such that

\displaystyle\|\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s}[\delta A_{s,u,t}]\|_{L_{m}}\leqslant\Gamma_{3}|t-s|+\Gamma_{4}|t-s|^{1+\varepsilon_{4}},

(4.11)

for every
(s,t)\in\Delta_{S,T}
and
u:=(s+t)/2
.
Then there exist a constant
C=C(\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2},\varepsilon_{4},m)
independent of
S
,
T
such that
for every
(s,t)\in\Delta_{S,T}
we have

\|\mathcal{A}_{t}-\mathcal{A}_{s}-A_{s,t}\|_{L_{m}}\leqslant C\Gamma_{3}\left(1+|\log\frac{\Gamma_{1}T^{\varepsilon_{1}}}{\Gamma_{3}}|\right)(t-s)+C\Gamma_{2}(t-s)^{\frac{1}{2}+\varepsilon_{2}}+C\Gamma_{4}(t-s)^{1+\varepsilon_{4}}.

(4.12)

Finally, our last sewing lemma is stochastic sewing lemma with random controls. It is interesting to compare it with deterministic sewing lemma with controls

[
FZ18
, Theorem 2.2]
. We would need the following definition.

Definition 4.6
.

Let
\lambda
be a measurable function
\Delta_{S,T}\times\Omega\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
. We say that
\lambda
is a
random control
if for any
S\leqslant s\leqslant u\leqslant t\leqslant T
one has

\lambda(s,u,\omega)+\lambda(u,t,\omega)\leqslant\lambda(s,t,\omega)\quad\text{a.s.}

Theorem 4.7

(Stochastic sewing lemma with random controls)
.

Let
m\in[2,\infty)
. Let
\lambda
be a random control. Assume that there exist constants
\Gamma_{1},\alpha_{1},\beta_{1}\geqslant 0
, such that
\alpha_{1}+\beta_{1}>1
and

|\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{u}\delta A_{s,u,t}|\leqslant\Gamma_{1}|t-s|^{\alpha_{1}}\lambda(s,t)^{\beta_{1}}\quad\text{a.s.}

(4.13)

for every
(s,t)\in\Delta_{S,T}
and
u:=(s+t)/2
.
Assume further that condition (
4.3
) is satisfied with
n=m
,
\alpha_{2}=\beta_{2}=0
,
\varepsilon_{2}>0
and condition (
4.4
) holds.

Then there exists a map
B\colon\Delta_{S,T}\to{L_{m}}
and a constant
C>0
, such that
B
is a functional of
\left((s,t)\mapsto\delta A_{s,(s+t)/2,t}\right)
and for every
(s,t)\in\Delta_{S,T}

|\mathcal{A}_{t}-\mathcal{A}_{s}-A_{s,t}|\leqslant C\Gamma_{1}|t-s|^{\alpha_{1}}\lambda(s,t)^{\beta_{1}}+B_{s,t}\quad\text{a.s.}

(4.14)

and

\|B_{s,t}\|_{L_{m}}\leqslant C\Gamma_{2}|t-s|^{\frac{1}{2}+\varepsilon_{2}}.

(4.15)

4.2
Proofs of stochastic sewing lemmas

The proofs of the results from
Section

4.1
make use of the following common notation.
For each integer
k\geqslant 0
and each
(s,t)\in\Delta_{S,T}
with
s>0
, let
\pi^{k}_{[s,t]}=\{s=t^{k}_{0}<t^{k}_{1}<\cdots<t^{k}_{2^{k}}=t\}
be the dyadic partition of
[s,t]
.

For each
k,i
,
u^{k}_{i}
denotes the midpoint of
[t^{k}_{i},t^{k}_{i+1}]
.
Define

A^{k}_{s,t}:=\sum_{i=0}^{2^{k}-1}A_{t^{k}_{i},t^{k}_{i+1}}\,.

Let
(s,t)\in\Delta_{S,T}
be fixed.
For every
k\geqslant 0
, we have

\displaystyle A^{k+1}_{s,t}-A^{k}_{s,t}=\sum_{i=0}^{2^{k}-1}\delta A_{t^{k}_{i},u^{k}_{i},t^{k}_{i+1}}=I^{k}_{s,t}+J^{k}_{s,t}

(4.16)

where

\displaystyle I^{k}_{s,t}=\sum_{i=0}^{2^{k}-1}\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{t^{k}_{i}}\delta A_{t^{k}_{i},u^{k}_{i},t^{k}_{i+1}}\,\textrm{ and }\,J^{k}_{s,t}=\sum_{i=0}^{2^{k}-1}\left(\delta A_{t^{k}_{i},u^{k}_{i},t^{k}_{i+1}}-\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{t^{k}_{i}}\delta A_{t^{k}_{i},u^{k}_{i},t^{k}_{i+1}}\right)\,.

(4.17)

Proof of
4.1
.

We estimate
I^{k}_{s,t}
by triangle inequality and condition (
4.2
),

\displaystyle\|I^{k}_{s,t}\|_{L_{n}}

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{i=0}^{2^{k}-1}\|\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{t^{k}_{i}}\delta A_{t^{k}_{i},u^{k}_{i},t^{k}_{i+1}}\|_{L_{n}}

\displaystyle\leqslant\Gamma_{1}\sum_{i=0}^{2^{k}-1}(u^{k}_{i}-S)^{-\alpha_{1}}(T-u^{k}_{i})^{-\beta_{1}}(t^{k}_{i+1}-t^{k}_{i})^{1+\varepsilon_{1}}\,.

Using (
B.6
), it is easy to see that

\sum_{i=0}^{2^{k}-1}(u^{k}_{i}-S)^{-\alpha_{1}}(T-u^{k}_{i})^{-\beta_{1}}(t^{k}_{i+1}-t^{k}_{i})\leqslant 2^{1+\alpha_{1}}\int_{s}^{t}(r-S)^{-\alpha_{1}}(T-r)^{-\beta_{1}}dr\,,

which implies

\displaystyle\|\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s}I^{k}_{s,t}\|_{L_{n}}\leqslant\|I^{k}_{s,t}\|_{L_{n}}\leqslant 2^{-k\varepsilon_{1}}2^{1+\alpha_{1}}\Gamma_{1}\left(\int_{s}^{t}(r-S)^{-\alpha_{1}}(T-r)^{-\beta_{1}}dr\right)(t-s)^{\varepsilon_{1}}\,.

(4.18)

To estimate
J^{k}_{s,t}
, we observe that it is a sum of martingale differences and use the
conditional Burkholder–Davis–Gundy (BDG) inequality (see, e.g.,
[
CF16
, Proposition 27]
) to obtain

\displaystyle\|J^{k}_{s,t}\|_{L_{m}|\mathcal{F}_{S}}

\displaystyle\leqslant\kappa_{m}\left(\sum_{i=0}^{2^{k}-1}\|\delta A_{t^{k}_{i},u^{k}_{i},t^{k}_{i+1}}-\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{t^{k}_{i}}\delta A_{t^{k}_{i},u^{k}_{i},t^{k}_{i+1}}\|_{L_{m}|\mathcal{F}_{S}}^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\leqslant 2\kappa_{m}\left(\sum_{i=0}^{2^{k}-1}\|\delta A_{t^{k}_{i},u^{k}_{i},t^{k}_{i+1}}\|_{L_{m}|\mathcal{F}_{S}}^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\,,

where
\kappa_{m}
is the constant from the conditional BDG inequality.
Then, we use the Minkowski inequality, condition (
4.3
) and similar reasoning as above, to see that

\displaystyle\|\|J^{k}_{s,t}\|_{L_{m}|\mathcal{F}_{S}}\|_{L_{n}}

\displaystyle\leqslant 2\kappa_{m}\left(\sum_{i=0}^{2^{k}-1}\|\|\delta A_{t^{k}_{i},u^{k}_{i},t^{k}_{i+1}}\|_{L_{m}|\mathcal{F}_{S}}\|_{L_{n}}^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\leqslant 2\kappa_{m}\left(\Gamma_{2}^{2}\sum_{i=0}^{2^{k}-1}(u^{k}_{i}-S)^{-2\alpha_{2}}(T-u^{k}_{i})^{-2\beta_{2}}(t^{k}_{i+1}-t^{k}_{i})^{1+2\varepsilon_{2}}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\leqslant 2^{-k\varepsilon_{2}}2^{\frac{3}{2}+\alpha_{2}}\kappa_{m}\Gamma_{2}\left(\int_{s}^{t}(r-S)^{-2\alpha_{2}}(T-r)^{-2\beta_{2}}dr\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}(t-s)^{\varepsilon_{2}}.

Hence, we have shown that

\displaystyle\|\|A^{k+1}_{s,t}-A^{k}_{s,t}\|_{L_{m}|\mathcal{F}_{S}}\|_{L_{n}}

\displaystyle\leqslant 2^{-k\varepsilon_{1}}4\Gamma_{1}\eta_{S,T}^{(\alpha_{1},\beta_{1})}(s,t)(t-s)^{\varepsilon_{1}}

\displaystyle+2^{-k\varepsilon_{2}}8\kappa_{m}\Gamma_{2}|\eta_{S,T}^{(2\alpha_{2},2\beta_{2})}(s,t)|^{\frac{1}{2}}(t-s)^{\varepsilon_{2}}\,.

This implies that

\|\|A^{k}_{s,t}-A_{s,t}\|_{L_{m}|\mathcal{F}_{S}}\|_{L_{n}}\leqslant C\Gamma_{1}\eta_{S,T}^{(\alpha_{1},\beta_{1})}(s,t)(t-s)^{\varepsilon_{1}}+C\Gamma_{2}|\eta_{S,T}^{(2\alpha_{2},2\beta_{2})}(s,t)|^{\frac{1}{2}}(t-s)^{\varepsilon_{2}}

(4.19)

for some constant
C=C(\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2},m)
.
By sending
k\to\infty
, using (
4.4
) and Fatou’s lemma, we obtain (
4.5
).
We observe that
\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{S}J^{k}_{s,t}=0
, the relation (
4.16
) also yields
\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{S}(A^{k+1}_{s,t}-A^{k}_{s,t})=\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{S}I^{k}_{s,t}
.
In view of the estimate (
4.18
), we obtain

\|\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{S}(A^{k+1}_{s,t}-A^{k}_{s,t})\|_{L_{n}}\leqslant 2^{-k\varepsilon_{1}}2^{1+\alpha_{1}}\Gamma_{1}\eta_{S,T}^{(\alpha_{1},\beta_{1})}(s,t)(t-s)^{\varepsilon_{1}}.

This yields
