\displaystyle\,\,=\Bigl|\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s}\int_{u}^{t}\!\!\int_{D}p_{T-r}(x,y)\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{u}[h_{r,y}(V_{r}(y))+l_{r,y}(V_{r}(y))]\,dydr\Bigr|

\displaystyle\,\,\leqslant C\int_{u}^{t}\!\!\int_{D}p_{T-r}(x,y)[(r-u)^{\frac{\beta-1-\lambda}{4}-\frac{1}{4q}}\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s}\|h_{r,y}\|_{\mathcal{B}^{\beta-1-\lambda}_{q}}+(r-u)^{\frac{\beta-1}{4}-\frac{1}{4q}}\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s}\|l_{r,y}\|_{\mathcal{B}^{\beta-1}_{q}}]\,dydr.

(6.16)

We see that by (
A.6
) and (
B.8
)

\displaystyle\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s}\|h_{r,y}\|_{\mathcal{B}^{\beta-1-\lambda}_{q}}

\displaystyle\leqslant\|b_{k}\|_{\mathcal{B}^{\beta}_{q}}|P_{r-s}\psi_{s}(y)-P_{r-s}\varphi_{s}(y)|^{\lambda}\,\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s}|P_{r-s}\psi_{s}(y)-P_{r-u}\psi_{u}(y)|

\displaystyle\leqslant\|b_{k}\|_{\mathcal{B}^{\beta}_{q}}|P_{r-s}z_{s}(y)|^{\lambda}\,\|\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s}|P_{r-s}\psi_{s}(y)-P_{r-u}\psi_{u}(y)|\|_{{L_{\infty}}}

\displaystyle\leqslant\|b_{k}\|_{\mathcal{B}^{\beta}_{q}}|P_{r-s}z_{s}(y)|^{\lambda}\,\sup_{z\in{D}}\|\|P_{u-s}\psi_{s}(z)-\psi_{u}(z)\|_{{L_{1}}|\mathcal{F}_{s}}\|_{{L_{\infty}}}

\displaystyle\leqslant\|b_{k}\|_{\mathcal{B}^{\beta}_{q}}[\psi]_{{\mathcal{C}^{3/4,0}L_{m,\infty}({[0,T_{0}]})}}|P_{r-s}z_{s}(y)|^{\lambda}(t-s)^{\frac{3}{4}}.

This, (
B.8
) and the fact that
\|b_{k}\|_{\mathcal{B}^{\beta}_{q}}\leqslant\|b\|_{\mathcal{B}^{\beta}_{q}}
imply

\|\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s}\|h_{r,y}\|_{\mathcal{B}^{\beta-1-\lambda}_{q}}\|_{{L_{m}}}\leqslant C\|b\|_{\mathcal{B}^{\beta}_{q}}[\psi]_{{\mathcal{C}^{3/4,0}L_{m,\infty}({[0,1]})}}\|z\|_{{\mathcal{C}^{0,0}L_{m}({[S,T]})}}^{\lambda}(t-s)^{\frac{3}{4}}.

(6.17)

In a similar manner, by (
A.5
) and (
B.8
) we see that

\displaystyle\|\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s}\|l_{r,y}\|_{\mathcal{B}^{\beta-1}_{q}}\|_{{L_{m}}}

\displaystyle\leqslant\|b_{k}\|_{\mathcal{B}^{\beta}_{q}}\|P_{r-u}z_{u}(y)-P_{r-s}z_{s}(y)\|_{{L_{m}}}

\displaystyle\leqslant\|b\|_{\mathcal{B}^{\beta}_{q}}\sup_{y\in{D}}\|z_{u}(y)-P_{u-s}z_{s}(y)\|_{{L_{m}}}.

(6.18)

Thus,

\displaystyle\|\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s}\|l_{r,y}\|_{\mathcal{B}^{\beta-1}_{q}}\|_{{L_{m}}}

\displaystyle\leqslant\|b\|_{\mathcal{B}^{\beta}_{q}}(\sup_{y\in{D}}\|\varphi_{u}(y)-P_{u-s}\varphi_{s}(y)\|_{{L_{m}}}+\sup_{y\in{D}}\|\psi_{u}(y)-P_{u-s}\psi_{s}(y)\|_{{L_{m}}})

\displaystyle\leqslant\|b\|_{\mathcal{B}^{\beta}_{q}}([\psi]_{{\mathcal{C}^{3/4,0}L_{m}({[S,T]})}}+[\varphi]_{{\mathcal{C}^{3/4,0}L_{m}({[S,T]})}})(t-s)^{\frac{3}{4}}

\displaystyle\leqslant\|b\|_{\mathcal{B}^{\beta}_{q}}([\psi]_{{\mathcal{C}^{3/4,0}L_{m,\infty}({[0,T_{0}]})}}+[\varphi]_{{\mathcal{C}^{3/4,0}L_{m}({[S,T]})}})(t-s)^{\frac{3}{4}}.

Substituting this together with (
6.17
) into (
6.2
) and taking
\lambda=0
, we obtain

\|\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s}\delta A_{s,u,t}\|_{{L_{m}}}\leqslant C(T_{0},\|b\|_{\mathcal{B}^{\beta}_{q}},[\psi]_{{\mathcal{C}^{3/4,0}L_{m,\infty}({[0,T_{0}]})}},[\varphi]_{{\mathcal{C}^{3/4,0}L_{m,\infty}({[0,T_{0}]})}})(t-s)^{\frac{5}{4}},

where we used the fact that, by assumptions,
\beta\geqslant-1+1/q
. Thus, condition (
4.2
)
holds.

Now let us verify (
4.3
).
Fix
\varepsilon\in(0,1+\beta)
. Let us apply
6.2
(iii) with
f=b_{k}
,
\gamma=\beta
,
p=q
,
\lambda_{1}=\lambda=1
and
\lambda_{2}=\beta+1-\varepsilon
and

\displaystyle\kappa_{1}:=P_{u-s}\psi_{s},

\displaystyle\kappa_{2}:=P_{u-s}\varphi_{s},

\displaystyle\kappa_{3}:=\psi_{u},

\displaystyle\kappa_{4}:=\varphi_{u}.

One can see that the functions
\kappa_{1}
and
\kappa_{2}
are
\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{R})\times\mathcal{F}_{s}
measurable, and the functions
\kappa_{3}
and
\kappa_{4}
are
\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{R})\times\mathcal{F}_{u}
measurable, exactly as required by the conditions of
6.2
(iii). Further, we see that
\kappa_{i}\in{\mathbf{B}L_{m}}
,
i=1,..,4
, thanks to (
5.28
) and (
B.8
). Finally, conditions (
6.8
) and (
6.10
) are satisfied thanks to our choice of
\lambda
,
\lambda_{1}
,
\lambda_{2}
. Therefore all the conditions of
6.2
(iii) are met. Recall the notation
z:=\psi-\varphi
and note that by (
B.8
)

\displaystyle\|\kappa_{1}-\kappa_{2}\|_{{\mathbf{B}L_{m}}}

\displaystyle\leqslant\|z_{s}\|_{{\mathbf{B}L_{m}}}\leqslant\|z\|_{{\mathcal{C}^{0,0}L_{m}({[S,T]})}},

\displaystyle\sup_{y\in{D}}\|\|\kappa_{1}(y)-\kappa_{3}(y)\|_{{L_{m}}|\mathcal{F}_{s}}\|_{{L_{\infty}}}

\displaystyle\leqslant[\psi]_{{\mathcal{C}^{3/4,0}L_{m,\infty}({[0,T_{0}]})}}(t-u)^{3/4},

\displaystyle\|\kappa_{1}-\kappa_{2}-\kappa_{3}+\kappa_{4}\|_{{\mathbf{B}L_{m}}}

\displaystyle\leqslant\|z_{u}-P_{u-s}z_{s}\|_{{\mathbf{B}L_{m}}}

\displaystyle\leqslant\|R_{s,u}\|_{{\mathbf{B}L_{m}}}+\|H^{\psi}_{s,u}-H^{\varphi}_{s,u}\|_{{\mathbf{B}L_{m}}}

\displaystyle\leqslant\|R\|_{{\mathcal{C}^{\tau,0}L_{m}({[S,T]})}}(t-u)^{\tau}+C\|b\|_{\mathcal{B}^{\gamma}_{p}}\|z\|_{{\mathcal{C}^{0,0}L_{m}({[S,T]})}}(t-u)^{\frac{1}{2}},

where the last inequality follows from (
5.30
) and the definition of the norm
\|\cdot\|_{{\mathcal{C}^{\tau,0}L_{m}({[S,T]})}}
given in (
5.27
).
Substituting all this into (
3
) and using the fact that
\|b_{k}\|_{\mathcal{B}^{\beta}_{q}}\leqslant\|b\|_{\mathcal{B}^{\beta}_{q}}
, one gets

\displaystyle\|\delta A_{s,u,t}\|_{{L_{m}}}\leqslant

\displaystyle C\|b_{k}\|_{\mathcal{B}^{\beta}_{q}}\|z\|_{{\mathcal{C}^{0,0}L_{m}({[S,T]})}}[\psi]_{{\mathcal{C}^{3/4,0}L_{m,\infty}({[0,T_{0}]})}}^{\gamma+1-\varepsilon}(t-u)^{\frac{5}{4}+\frac{3\beta}{4}-\frac{1}{4q}-\frac{\varepsilon}{2}}

\displaystyle\phantom{\leqslant}+C\|b_{k}\|_{\mathcal{B}^{\beta}_{q}}\|R\|_{{\mathcal{C}^{\tau,0}L_{m}({[S,T]})}}(t-u)^{\frac{1}{2}+\tau}

\displaystyle\phantom{\leqslant}+C\|b_{k}\|_{\mathcal{B}^{\beta}_{q}}\|b\|_{\mathcal{B}^{\beta}_{q}}\|z\|_{{\mathcal{C}^{0,0}L_{m}({[S,T]})}}(t-u)

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle C(T_{0},\|b\|_{\mathcal{B}^{\beta}_{q}},[\psi]_{{\mathcal{C}^{3/4,0}L_{m,\infty}({[0,T_{0}]})}})\bigl(\|z\|_{{\mathcal{C}^{0,0}L_{m}({[S,T]})}}+\|R\|_{{\mathcal{C}^{\tau,0}L_{m}({[S,T]})}}\bigr)(t-s)^{\frac{1}{2}+\delta},

(6.19)

where we have also used that
\beta-1/q\geqslant-1
and we put
\delta:=(\frac{3}{4}+\frac{3\beta}{4}-\frac{1}{4q}-\frac{\varepsilon}{2})\wedge\frac{1}{2}
. Since
\beta>-1+1/(3q)
, we see that there exists
\varepsilon=\varepsilon(\beta,q)>0
small enough such that
\delta>0
. From now on till the end of the proof we fix such
\varepsilon
. Then (
6.2
) implies that condition (
4.3
) is satisfied.

Now let us check (
4.4
). We have

\displaystyle\Bigl\|\mathcal{A}_{t}-\sum_{i=0}^{k-1}A_{t_{i},t_{i+1}}\Bigr\|_{{L_{1}}}

\displaystyle\quad\leqslant\sum_{i=0}^{k-1}\int_{t_{i}}^{t_{i+1}}\!\!\!\int_{D}p_{T-r}(x,y)\bigl\|b_{k}(V_{r}(y)+\psi_{r}(y))-b_{k}(V_{r}(y)+P_{r-{t_{i}}}\psi_{t_{i}}(y))\bigr\|_{{L_{1}}}\,dydr

\displaystyle\quad\phantom{\leqslant}+\sum_{i=0}^{k-1}\int_{t_{i}}^{t_{i+1}}\!\!\!\int_{D}p_{T-r}(x,y)\bigl\|b_{k}(V_{r}(y)+\varphi_{r}(y))-b_{k}(V_{r}(y)+P_{r-{t_{i}}}\varphi_{t_{i}}(y))\bigr\|_{{L_{1}}}\,dydr

\displaystyle\quad\leqslant\|b_{k}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{1}}\sum_{i=0}^{k-1}\int_{t_{i}}^{t_{i+1}}\!\!\!\int_{D}p_{T-r}(x,y)(\|\psi_{r}(y)-P_{r-{t_{i}}}\psi_{t_{i}}(y)\|_{{L_{1}}}+\|\varphi_{r}(y)-P_{r-{t_{i}}}\varphi_{t_{i}}(y)\|_{{L_{1}}})\,dydr

\displaystyle\quad\leqslant CT_{0}\|b_{k}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{1}}([\psi]_{{\mathcal{C}^{3/4,0}L_{m}({[0,T_{0}]})}}+[\varphi]_{{\mathcal{C}^{3/4,0}L_{m}({[0,T_{0}]})}})|\Pi|^{3/4}.

This implies that
|\mathcal{A}_{t}-\sum_{i=0}^{k-1}A_{t_{i},t_{i+1}}|
converges to
0
in
{L_{1}}
as
k\to\infty
. Hence (
4.4
) holds.

Finally, let us check (
4.11
). Substituting (
6.17
) and (
6.2
) into (
6.2
) and taking
\lambda=1
, we obtain

\displaystyle\|\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s}\delta A_{s,u,t}\|_{{L_{m}}}

\displaystyle\leqslant C(1+T_{0})\|b\|_{\mathcal{B}^{\beta}_{q}}[\psi]_{{\mathcal{C}^{3/4,0}L_{m,\infty}({[0,T_{0}]})}}\|z\|_{{\mathcal{C}^{0,0}L_{m}({[S,T]})}}(t-s)

\displaystyle\phantom{\leqslant}+C(1+T_{0})\|b\|_{\mathcal{B}^{\beta}_{q}}(t-s)^{\frac{1}{2}}(\sup_{y\in{D}}\|R_{s,u}(y)\|+\sup_{y\in{D}}\|H^{\psi}_{s,u}(y)-H^{\varphi}_{s,u}(y)\|)

\displaystyle\leqslant C(1+T_{0})\|b\|_{\mathcal{B}^{\beta}_{q}}(1+[\psi]_{{\mathcal{C}^{3/4,0}L_{m,\infty}({[0,T_{0}]})}})\|z\|_{{\mathcal{C}^{0,0}L_{m}({[S,T]})}}(t-s)

\displaystyle\phantom{\leqslant}+C(1+T_{0})\|b\|_{\mathcal{B}^{\beta}_{q}}\|R\|_{{\mathcal{C}^{\tau,0}L_{m}({[S,T]})}}(t-s)^{\frac{1}{2}+\tau},

where the last inequality follows from (
5.30
). Recalling that, by assumptions, we have
\tau>1/2
, we see that (
4.11
) holds.

Thus all the conditions of
4.5
are met. We deduce from (
4.12
)

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{A}^{k}_{T}-\mathcal{A}^{k}_{S}-A^{k}_{S,T}\|_{L_{m}}

\displaystyle\leqslant C\|z\|_{{\mathcal{C}^{0,0}L_{m}({[S,T]})}}(1+|\log{\|z\|_{{\mathcal{C}^{0,0}L_{m}({[S,T]})}}}|)(T-S)

\displaystyle\phantom{\leqslant}+C(\|z\|_{{\mathcal{C}^{0,0}L_{m}({[S,T]})}}+\|R\|_{{\mathcal{C}^{\tau,0}L_{m}({[S,T]})}})(T-S)^{\frac{1}{2}+\delta}

\displaystyle\phantom{\leqslant}+C\|R\|_{{\mathcal{C}^{\tau,0}L_{m}({[S,T]})}}(T-S)^{\frac{1}{2}+\tau}

\displaystyle\leqslant C\|z\|_{{\mathcal{C}^{0,0}L_{m}({[S,T]})}}|\log{\|z\|_{{\mathcal{C}^{0,0}L_{m}({[S,T]})}}}|(T-S)

\displaystyle\phantom{\leqslant}+C(\|z\|_{{\mathcal{C}^{0,0}L_{m}({[S,T]})}}+\|R\|_{{\mathcal{C}^{\tau,0}L_{m}({[S,T]})}})(T-S)^{\frac{1}{2}+\delta},

(6.20)

where
C=C(T_{0},\|b\|_{\mathcal{B}^{\beta}_{q}},[\psi]_{{\mathcal{C}^{3/4,0}L_{m,\infty}({[0,T_{0}]})}},\tau,\delta)

is independent of
x
,
S
,
T
.

Now let us pass to the limit in (
6.2
) as
k\to\infty
.
Recall the notation
K^{h;\sigma}
introduced in (
5.12
). Then it is easy to see that

\mathcal{A}^{k}_{T}-\mathcal{A}^{k}_{S}=K^{b_{k};u}_{T}(x)-K^{b_{k};v}_{T}(x)-P_{T-S}[K^{b_{k};u}_{S}-K^{b_{k};v}_{S}](x).

Applying
Lemmas

5.6
and
5.5
we can conclude that

\lim_{k\to\infty}\mathcal{A}^{k}_{T}-\mathcal{A}^{k}_{S}-A^{k}_{S,T}=z_{T}(x)-P_{T-S}z_{S}(x)-(H^{\psi}_{S,T}(x)-H^{\varphi}_{S,T}(x))=R_{S,T}(x),

in probability. Inequality (
5.34
) follows now from (
6.2
) by Fatou’s lemma. Inequality (
5.7
) follows immediately from (
5.34
) and (
5.30
).
∎

Appendix A
Useful results on Besov spaces

We give a brief summary on nonhomogeneous Besov space which is sufficient for our purpose. For a more detailed account on the topic, we refer to
[
BCD11
, Chapter 2]
. Let
\varsigma,\varpi
be the radial functions which are given by
[
BCD11
, Proposition 2.10]
.
We note that
\varsigma
is supported on a ball while
\varpi
is supported on an annulus. Let
h_{-1}
and
h
respectively be the inverse Fourier transform of
\varsigma
and
\varpi
. The nonhomogeneous dyadic blocks
\Delta_{j}
are defined by

\Delta_{-1}f=\int_{\mathbb{R}}h_{-1}(y)f(\cdot-y)dy\quad\textrm{and}\quad\Delta_{j}f=\int_{\mathbb{R}}h_{j}(y)f(\cdot-y)dy\quad\textrm{for}\quad j\geqslant 0,

where
h_{j}(y)=2^{j}h(2^{j}y)
,
j\geqslant 0
.

Definition A.1
.

Let
\gamma\in\mathbb{R}
and
1\leqslant p\leqslant\infty
. The nonhomogeneous Besov space
\mathcal{B}^{\gamma}_{p}=\mathcal{B}^{\gamma}_{p,\infty}(\mathbb{R})
consists of all tempered distributions
f
such that

\|f\|_{\mathcal{B}^{\gamma}_{p}}:=\sup_{j\geqslant-1}2^{j\gamma}\|\Delta_{j}f\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R})}<\infty.

For a distribution
f
in
\mathcal{B}^{\gamma}_{p}
, we note that
\Delta_{j}f
is a smooth function for each
j\geqslant-1
. In addition, the Fourier transform of
\Delta_{-1}f
is supported on a ball
\mathcal{B}
while for
j\geqslant 1
the Fourier transform of
\Delta_{j}f
is supported on the annulus
2^{j}\mathcal{N}\subset 2^{j}\mathcal{B}
for some annulus
\mathcal{N}
.

To obtain various properties of Besov spaces, we will make use of the following Bernstein’s inequalities.
Let
f
be a function in
L_{p}(\mathbb{R})
. For every integer
k\geqslant 0
, every
\lambda>0
and
t>0
we have

\displaystyle\supp Ff\subset\lambda\mathcal{B}\Rightarrow\|\nabla^{k}f\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R})}\leqslant C^{k+1}\lambda^{k}\|f\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R})},

(A.1)

\displaystyle\supp Ff\subset\lambda\mathcal{N}\Rightarrow\|G_{t}f\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R})}\leqslant Ce^{-ct\lambda^{2}}\|f\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R})},

(A.2)

\displaystyle\supp Ff\subset\lambda\mathcal{B}\Rightarrow\|G_{t}f-f\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R})}\leqslant C(t\lambda^{2}\wedge 1)\|f\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R})},

(A.3)

where
Ff
denotes the Fourier transform of
f
.
We refer to
[
BCD11
, Lemmas 2.1 and 2.4]
for proofs of (
A.1
) and (
A.2
). For a proof of (
A.3
), we refer to
[
MW17
, Lemma 4]
.

Lemma A.2
.

Let
f
be a tempered distribution on
\mathbb{R}
,
\gamma\in\mathbb{R}
,
p\in[1,\infty]
. Then for any
a,a_{1},a_{2},a_{3}\in\mathbb{R}
,
\alpha,\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\in[0,1]
one has

\displaystyle\|f(a+\cdot)\|_{\mathcal{B}^{\gamma}_{p}}

\displaystyle=\|f\|_{\mathcal{B}^{\gamma}_{p}},

(A.4)

\displaystyle\|f(a_{1}+\cdot)-f(a_{2}+\cdot)\|_{\mathcal{B}^{\gamma}_{p}}

\displaystyle\leqslant C|a_{1}-a_{2}|^{\alpha}\|f\|_{\mathcal{B}^{\gamma+\alpha}_{p}},

(A.5)

and

\|f(a_{1}+\cdot)-f(a_{2}+\cdot)-f(a_{3}+\cdot)+f(a_{3}+a_{2}-a_{1}+\cdot)\|_{\mathcal{B}^{\gamma}_{p}}\\
\leqslant C|a_{1}-a_{2}|^{\alpha_{1}}|a_{1}-a_{3}|^{\alpha_{2}}\|f\|_{\mathcal{B}^{\gamma+\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}}_{p}}.

(A.6)

Proof.

Since
\|\Delta_{j}f(a+\cdot)\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R})}=\|\Delta_{j}f(\cdot)\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R})}
, (
A.4
) follows from
Definition

A.1
.

For each
j\geqslant-1
, applying mean value theorem and (
A.1
), we have

\displaystyle\|\Delta_{j}(f(a_{1}+\cdot)-f(a_{2}+\cdot))\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R})}\leqslant C|a_{1}-a_{2}|2^{j}\|\Delta_{j}f\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R})}.

By triangle inequality, it is evident that

\displaystyle\|\Delta_{j}(f(a_{1}+\cdot)-f(a_{2}+\cdot))\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R})}\leqslant 2\|\Delta_{j}f\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R})}.

Hence, by interpolation, we obtain

\|\Delta_{j}(f(a_{1}+\cdot)-f(a_{2}+\cdot))\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R})}\leqslant C|a_{1}-a_{2}|^{\alpha}2^{j\alpha}\|\Delta_{j}f\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R})}.

(A.7)

In view of
Definition

A.1
, we multiply both sides with
2^{j\gamma}
and take supremum over
j\geqslant-1
to obtain (
A.5
).

Similarly, using mean value theorem and (
A.1
), we have

\|\Delta_{j}(f(a_{1}+\cdot)-f(a_{2}+\cdot)-f(a_{3}+\cdot)+f(a_{3}+a_{2}-a_{1}+\cdot))\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R})}\\
\leqslant C|a_{1}-a_{2}||a_{1}-a_{3}|2^{2j}\|\Delta_{j}f\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R})}

and

\|\Delta_{j}(f(a_{1}+\cdot)-f(a_{2}+\cdot)-f(a_{3}+\cdot)+f(a_{3}+a_{2}-a_{1}+\cdot))\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R})}\\
\leqslant C\min(1,|a_{1}-a_{2}|2^{j},|a_{1}-a_{3}|2^{j})\|\Delta_{j}f\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R})}.

Interpolating between these inequalities, we obtain

\|\Delta_{j}(f(a_{1}+\cdot)-f(a_{2}+\cdot)-f(a_{3}+\cdot)+f(a_{3}+a_{2}-a_{1}+\cdot))\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R})}\\
\leqslant C|a_{1}-a_{2}|^{\alpha_{1}}|a_{1}-a_{3}|^{\alpha_{2}}2^{j(\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2})}\|\Delta_{j}f\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R})}.

We multiply both sides with
2^{j\gamma}
and take supremum over
j\geqslant-1
to obtain (
A.6
).
∎

Lemma A.3
.

For every
f
in
\mathcal{B}^{\gamma}_{p}
,
\gamma\in\mathbb{R}
,
p\in[1,\infty]
, we have

1.

provided that
\gamma<0
,
\|G_{t}f\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R})}\leqslant C_{\gamma}\|f\|_{\mathcal{B}^{\gamma}_{p}}t^{\frac{\gamma}{2}}
for all
t>0
;

2.

\lim_{t\to 0}G_{t}f=f
in
\mathcal{B}^{\bar{\gamma}}_{p}
for every
\bar{\gamma}<\gamma
;

3.

\sup_{t>0}\|G_{t}f\|_{\mathcal{B}^{\gamma}_{p}}\leqslant\|f\|_{\mathcal{B}^{\gamma}_{p}}
and

4.

provided that
\gamma-\frac{1}{p}<0
,
\|G_{t}f\|_{\mathcal{C}^{1}}\leqslant C\|f\|_{\mathcal{B}^{\gamma}_{p}}t^{\frac{1}{2}(\gamma-\frac{1}{p}-1)}
for all
t>0
.

Proof.

(i) Fix
\gamma<0
.
We have by (
A.2
) that there exists some constant
c>0
such that for every
j\geqslant 0

\displaystyle\|G_{t}(\Delta_{j}f)\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R})}\leqslant Ce^{-ct2^{2j}}\|\Delta_{j}f\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R})}\leqslant Ce^{-ct2^{2j}}2^{-j\gamma}\|f\|_{\mathcal{B}^{\gamma}_{p}}.

For
j=-1
, we use the trivial bounds

\displaystyle\|G_{t}(\Delta_{-1}f)\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R})}\leqslant\|\Delta_{-1}f\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R})}\leqslant C\|f\|_{\mathcal{B}^{\gamma}_{p}}.

Since
G_{t}(\Delta_{j}f)=\Delta_{j}(G_{t}f)
, we have

\sum_{j\geqslant-1}\|\Delta_{j}(G_{t}f)\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R})}\leqslant C\|f\|_{\mathcal{B}^{\gamma}_{p}}\sum_{j\geqslant-1}e^{-ct2^{2j}}2^{-j\gamma}.

(A.8)

Clearly, if
j\leqslant x\leqslant j+1
,
j\geqslant-1
, then
e^{-ct2^{2j}}2^{-j\gamma}\leqslant e^{-ct2^{2x}}2^{-(x+1)\gamma}
. Therefore,

\sum_{j\geqslant-1}e^{-ct2^{2j}}2^{-j\gamma}\leqslant\int_{-1}^{\infty}e^{-ct2^{2x}}2^{-x\gamma}\,dx\leqslant Ct^{\frac{\gamma}{2}}\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-cy}y^{-\frac{\gamma}{2}-1}\,dy\leqslant Ct^{\frac{\gamma}{2}},

thanks to the assumption
\gamma<0
.
Using this and the fact that
\Delta_{j}(G_{t}f)=G_{t}(\Delta_{j}f)
, we derive from (
A.8
)

\displaystyle\sum_{j\geqslant-1}\|\Delta_{j}(G_{t}f)\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R})}\leqslant C\|f\|_{\mathcal{B}^{\gamma}_{p}}t^{\frac{\gamma}{2}}.

On the other hand, the identity
Id=\sum_{j\geqslant-1}\Delta_{j}
holds in distribution (
[
BCD11
, Proposition 2.12]
). The above estimate shows that it also holds in
L_{p}(\mathbb{R})
and hence,

\displaystyle\|G_{t}f\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R})}\leqslant\sum_{j\geqslant-1}\|\Delta_{j}(G_{t}f)\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R})}\leqslant C\|f\|_{\mathcal{B}^{\gamma}_{p}}t^{\frac{\gamma}{2}}

which implies (i).

(ii) From (
A.3
), we derive that for every
j\geqslant-1
and
\epsilon\in(0,1]

\|G_{t}(\Delta_{j}f)-\Delta_{j}f\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R})}\leqslant c(t2^{2j})^{\varepsilon}\|\Delta_{j}f\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R})}.

This yields

\displaystyle 2^{j(\gamma-2\varepsilon)}\|\Delta_{j}(G_{t}f-f)\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R})}\leqslant ct^{\varepsilon}2^{j\gamma}\|\Delta_{j}f\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R})}\leqslant ct^{\varepsilon}\|f\|_{\mathcal{B}^{\gamma}_{p}}.

By taking supremum over all
j\geqslant-1
in the above inequality, we see that

\lim_{t\downarrow 0}G_{t}f=f
in
\mathcal{B}^{\gamma-2\varepsilon}_{p}
. Since
\varepsilon
is arbitrary in
(0,1]
, this implies (ii) when
\bar{\gamma}\in[\gamma-2,\gamma)
. If
\bar{\gamma}<\gamma-2
, then

\|G_{t}f-f\|_{\mathcal{B}^{\bar{\gamma}}_{p}}\leqslant\|G_{t}f-f\|_{\mathcal{B}^{\gamma-2}_{p}}\to 0\,\,\text{as $t\to 0$}.

This proves (ii).

(iii) follows immediately from the inequality
\|G_{t}(\Delta_{j}f)\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R})}\leqslant\|\Delta_{j}f\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R})}
and
Definition

A.1
.

(iv) We write
G_{t}f=G_{t/2}G_{t/2}f
, apply standard heat kernel bounds and part (i) to get

\displaystyle\|G_{t}f\|_{\mathcal{C}^{1}}\leqslant Ct^{-\frac{1}{2}}\|G_{t/2}f\|_{L_{\infty}(\mathbb{R})}\leqslant Ct^{-\frac{1}{2}}t^{\frac{1}{2}(\gamma-\frac{1}{p})}\|f\|_{\mathcal{B}_{\infty}^{\gamma-1/p}}\leqslant Ct^{-\frac{1}{2}}t^{\frac{1}{2}(\gamma-\frac{1}{p})}\|f\|_{\mathcal{B}_{p}^{\gamma}},

where the last inequality follows from the embedding
\mathcal{B}^{\gamma}_{p}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{B}^{\gamma-1/p}_{\infty}
. This proves (iv). ∎

Recall that Schwarz distribution
\zeta^{-1}
is defined in (
2.9
) and
\zeta^{\alpha}_{+}
,
\zeta^{\alpha}_{-}
are defined in (
2.10
).

Lemma A.4
.

Let
\alpha\in(-1,0)
. Then we have

\zeta^{-1}\in\mathcal{B}^{-1+\frac{1}{p}}_{p}(\mathbb{R})\,\,\,\forall p\in(1,\infty]\quad\textrm{and}\quad\zeta^{\alpha}_{+},\zeta^{\alpha}_{-}\in\mathcal{B}^{\alpha+\frac{1}{p}}_{p}(\mathbb{R})\,\,\,\forall p\in(|\alpha|^{-1},\infty].

Proof.

By homogeneity, for every
j\geqslant 0
,
\Delta_{j}\zeta^{-1}(x)=2^{j}\Delta_{0}\zeta^{-1}(2^{j}x)
so that
\|\Delta_{j}\zeta^{-1}\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R})}=2^{j(1-\frac{1}{p})}\|\Delta_{0}\zeta^{-1}\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R})}
.
Note that

\displaystyle\Delta_{0}\zeta^{-1}(x)=\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{h(x+y)-h(x-y)}{y}dy

which is the Hilbert transform of
h
. It is known that Hilbert transform is bounded on
L_{p}(\mathbb{R})
for every
p\in(1,\infty)
. Hence, we have
\|\Delta_{0}\zeta^{-1}\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R})}\leqslant C\|h\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R})}
which is finite. Similarly, we see that
\|\Delta_{-1}\zeta^{-1}\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R})}
is finite. This shows that
\zeta^{-1}
belong to
\mathcal{B}^{-1+\frac{1}{p}}_{p}(\mathbb{R})
for every
p\in(1,\infty)
. That
\zeta^{-1}
belongs to
\mathcal{B}^{-1}_{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
follows from the Besov embedding
\mathcal{B}^{-1+\frac{1}{p}}_{p}(\mathbb{R})\hookrightarrow\mathcal{B}^{-1}_{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
.

Similarly, we see that
\|\Delta_{j}\zeta^{\alpha}_{\pm}\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R})}=2^{-j(\alpha+\frac{1}{p})}\|\Delta_{0}\zeta^{\alpha}_{\pm}\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R})}
. Let
q\in[1,\infty]
be such that
1+\frac{1}{p}=\gamma+\frac{1}{q}
. Applying the refined Young convolution inequality (
[
BCD11
, Theorem 1.5]
), we see that

\|\Delta_{0}\zeta^{\alpha}_{\pm}\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R})}\leqslant C\|h\|_{L_{q}(\mathbb{R})}
, which is finite.
That
\|\Delta_{-1}\zeta^{\alpha}_{\pm}\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R})}
is finite follows from the same argument.
∎

Lemma A.5
.

Let
f
and
c,c_{0}
be as in
2.12
(i).
For each
\lambda>0
, define
f_{\lambda}(x)=\lambda f(\lambda x)
. Then
f_{\lambda}
converges in
\mathcal{B}^{(-1+\frac{1}{p})_{-}}_{p}(\mathbb{R})
to the distribution
c\zeta^{-1}+c_{0}\delta_{0}
for every
p\in(1,\infty]
.

Proof.

By Besov embedding, it suffices to obtain the convergence in
\mathcal{B}^{(-1+\frac{1}{p})_{-}}_{p}(\mathbb{R})
for finite
p
. Fix
p\in(1,\infty)
,
\beta>0
.
Define a distribution

\chi:=c\zeta^{-1}+c_{0}\delta_{0}

and put
R(x):=f(x)-c/x
. Fix arbitrary
\varepsilon>0
. Choose
N>0
large enough so that

\int_{|x|>N}|R(x)|\,dx<\varepsilon\quad\textrm{and}\quad\Bigl|c_{0}-\int_{|x|<N}f(x)\,dx\Bigr|<\varepsilon.

(A.9)

This is possible thanks to the assumptions of the theorem.

For each
j\geqslant-1
, we write

\displaystyle\Delta_{j}(f_{\lambda}-\chi)(y)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}}f_{\lambda}(x)h_{j}(y-x)dx-c\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}\frac{h_{j}(y-x)-h_{j}(y+x)}{x}\,dx-c_{0}h_{j}(y)

\displaystyle=\lambda\int_{|x|>N/\lambda}R(\lambda x)h_{j}(y-x)\,dx-c\int_{0}^{N/\lambda}\frac{h_{j}(y-x)-h_{j}(y+x)}{x}\,dx

\displaystyle\phantom{=}+\lambda\int_{|x|<N/\lambda}f(\lambda x)(h_{j}(y-x)-h_{j}(y))\,dx-\Bigl(c_{0}-\int_{|x|<N}f(x)\,dx\Bigr)h_{j}(y)

\displaystyle=:I_{1,j}(y)+I_{2,j}(y)+I_{3,j}(y)+I_{4,j}(y).

(A.10)

We begin with
I_{1,j}
. Applying the integral Minkowski inequality, the change of variables
x^{\prime}=\lambda x
, and recalling (
A.9
), we deduce

\displaystyle\|I_{1,j}\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R})}

\displaystyle\leqslant\lambda\|h_{j}\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R})}\int_{|x|>N/\lambda}|R(\lambda x)|\,dx=\|h_{j}\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R})}\int_{|x|>N}|R(x)|\,dx

\displaystyle\leqslant 2^{j(1-\frac{1}{p})}(\|h\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R})}+\|h_{-1}\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R})})\varepsilon.

(A.11)

To bound
I_{2,j}
, we note that for
j\geqslant 1

\|I_{2,j}\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R})}^{p}=2^{j(p-1)}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\Bigl(\int_{0}^{2^{j}N/\lambda}\frac{h(y-x)-h(y+x)}{x}\,dx\Bigr)^{p}\,dy.

(A.12)

Introduce the following notation. For fixed
\delta>0
define the truncated Hilbert, Hilbert, and maximal Hilbert operators
H_{\delta}
,
H
, and
H^{*}
, correspondingly, by

\displaystyle H_{\delta}(\varphi)(y)=\int_{\delta}^{+\infty}\frac{\varphi(y-x)-\varphi(y-x)}{x}dx,\quad H(\varphi)=H_{0}(\varphi),

\displaystyle H^{*}(\varphi)(y)=\sup_{\delta>0}|H_{\delta}(\varphi)(y)|,

for each Schwarz function
\varphi
. With this notation in hand, we continue (
A.12
) in the following way

\|I_{2,j}\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R})}=2^{j(1-1/p)}\|H_{0}h-H_{2^{j}N/\lambda}h\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R})}.

(A.13)

Note also that the following bound holds:

\displaystyle\|I_{2,j}\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R})}

\displaystyle=2^{j(1-1/p)}(\|H_{0}h\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R})}+\|H_{2^{j}N/\lambda}h\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R})})

\displaystyle\leqslant 2^{j(1-1/p)}(\|H_{0}h\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R})}+\|H^{*}(h)\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R})}).

(A.14)

Similarly,

\|I_{2,-1}\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R})}=\|H_{0}h_{-1}-H_{N/\lambda}h_{-1}\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R})}.

(A.15)

To treat
I_{3,j}
we apply the integral Minkowski inequality and (
A.7
) with
f=\delta_{0}
. We get for any
\beta>0

\displaystyle\|I_{3,j}\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R})}

\displaystyle\leqslant\lambda\int_{|x|\leqslant N/\lambda}|f(\lambda x)|\|h_{j}(\cdot-x)-h_{j}(\cdot)\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R})}dx

\displaystyle\leqslant\lambda\int_{|x|\leqslant N/\lambda}|f(\lambda x)||x|^{\beta}2^{j\beta}\|h_{j}\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R})}\,dx

\displaystyle=\lambda^{-\beta}2^{j\beta}\|h_{j}\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R})}\int_{|x|\leqslant N}|f(x)||x|^{\beta}\,dx

\displaystyle\leqslant\lambda^{-\beta}2^{j(\beta+1-1/p)}(\|h\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R})}+\|h_{-1}\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R})})C_{N},

(A.16)

where
C_{N}:=N^{\beta}\int_{|x|\leqslant N}|f(x)|\,dx
.
Finally, it is easy to treat
I_{4,j}
. Using (
A.9
), we get

\displaystyle\|I_{4,j}\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R})}

\displaystyle\leqslant 2^{j(1-1/p)}(\|h\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R})}+\|h_{-1}\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R})})\varepsilon.

Now we substitute this, (
A
) and (
A
) into (
A
). To bound
I_{2,j}
we use (
A.15
) for
j=-1
, (
A.13
) for
j\in[1,J]
, and (
A
) for
j\geqslant J
; here
J
is a parameter to be fixed later. We finally obtain

\displaystyle\sup_{j\geqslant-1}2^{j(-1+\frac{1}{p}-\beta)}\|\Delta_{j}(f_{\lambda}-\chi)\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R})}

\displaystyle\qquad\leqslant C(\|h\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R})}+\|h_{-1}\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R})})(\varepsilon+\lambda^{-\beta}C_{N})+\|H_{0}h_{-1}-H_{N/\lambda}h_{-1}\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R})}

\displaystyle\qquad+\phantom{\leqslant}\sup_{\delta\leqslant 2^{J}N/\lambda}\|H_{0}h-H_{\delta}h\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R})}+2^{-J\beta}(\|H_{0}h\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R})}+\|H^{*}(h)\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R})}).

Sending first
\lambda\to\infty
, then
J\to\infty
, then
\varepsilon\downarrow 0
, we see that the left-hand side of the above inequality converges to
0
. Here we used the facts that

\|H_{0}h\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R})}<\infty
,
\|H^{*}(h)\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R})}<\infty
,

\|H_{0}h-H_{\delta}h\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R})}\to 0
,
\|H_{0}h_{-1}-H_{\delta}h_{-1}\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R})}\to 0
as
\delta\to 0
; all of them are established in
[
Gra14
, Theorem 5.1.12]
.

Hence,
\lim_{\lambda\to\infty}f_{\lambda}=c\zeta^{-1}+c_{0}\delta_{0}
in
\mathcal{B}^{-1+\frac{1}{p}-\beta}_{p}
. Since
\beta
is arbitrary in
(0,1)
, this implies the convergence in
\mathcal{B}^{(-1+\frac{1}{p})_{-}}_{p}
.
∎

Lemma A.6
.

Let
f
be a continuous function
\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
. Suppose that for some
\alpha\in(0,1)
,
c_{+},c_{-}\in\mathbb{R}
one has

\lim_{x\to+\infty}f(x)x^{\alpha}=c_{+}\quad\textrm{and}\quad\lim_{x\to-\infty}f(x)|x|^{\alpha}=c_{-}.

(A.17)

Let
f_{\lambda}(x):=\lambda^{\alpha}f(\lambda x)
, where
\lambda>0
,
x\in\mathbb{R}
. Then
f_{\lambda}
converges to
c_{-}\zeta^{-\alpha}_{-}+c_{+}\zeta^{-\alpha}_{+}
as
\lambda\to\infty
in
\mathcal{B}^{(-\alpha+\frac{1}{p})-}_{p}(\mathbb{R})
for every
p\in(\alpha^{-1},\infty]
.

Proof.

The proof is in the same spirit as the proof of
Lemma

A.5
.
Again, by the Besov embedding it is sufficient to consider the case of finite
p
. Fix
p\in(\alpha^{-1},\infty)
. Denote

\chi(x):=c_{-}\zeta^{-\alpha}_{-}+c_{+}\zeta^{-\alpha}_{+},\quad x\in\mathbb{R}

and put
R(x):=f(x)-\chi(x)
.

Take arbitrary
\varepsilon>0
. It follows from (
A.17
), that
there exists
N>0
such that

|R(x)||x|^{\alpha}<\varepsilon\text{ whenever }|x|>N.

(A.18)

For each
j\geqslant-1
, we write

\displaystyle\Delta_{j}(f_{\lambda}-\chi)(y)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}}(f_{\lambda}(x)-\chi(x))h_{j}(y-x)\,dx

\displaystyle=\lambda^{\alpha}\int_{\mathbb{R}}R(\lambda x)h_{j}(y-x)\,dx

\displaystyle=\lambda^{\alpha}\Bigl(\int_{|x|<N/\lambda}+\int_{|x|>N/\lambda}\Bigr)R(\lambda x)h_{j}(y-x)dx

\displaystyle=:I_{1}(y)+I_{2}(y).

(A.19)

Fix
\beta\in(0,1-\alpha)
. To bound
I_{1}
we apply Young inequality with
p^{\prime},q^{\prime}>1
such that
1/p^{\prime}+1/q^{\prime}=1+1/p
and the change of variables
x^{\prime}:=\lambda x
. We get

\displaystyle\|I_{1}\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R})}

\displaystyle\leqslant\lambda^{\alpha}\|h_{j}\|_{L_{p^{\prime}}(\mathbb{R})}\Bigl(\int_{|x|<N/\lambda}|R(\lambda x)|^{q^{\prime}}\,dx\Bigr)^{1/q^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\lambda^{-\frac{1}{q^{\prime}}+\alpha}\|h_{j}\|_{L_{p^{\prime}}(\mathbb{R})}\Bigl(\int_{|x|<N}|R(x)|^{q^{\prime}}\,dx\Bigr)^{1/q^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\leqslant C_{N}\lambda^{-\frac{1}{q^{\prime}}+\alpha}2^{j(1-\frac{1}{p^{\prime}})}(\|h\|_{L_{p^{\prime}}(\mathbb{R})}+\|h_{-1}\|_{L_{p^{\prime}}(\mathbb{R})})

where
C_{N}:=CN(1+\sup_{|x|\leqslant N}|f(x)|)
. By choosing now
1/q^{\prime}=\alpha+\beta<1
, we get
1/p^{\prime}=1+1/p-\alpha-\beta<1
and thus

\|I_{1}\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R})}\leqslant C_{N}\lambda^{-\beta}2^{j(\alpha+\beta-1/p)}(\|h\|_{L_{p^{\prime}}(\mathbb{R})}+\|h_{-1}\|_{L_{p^{\prime}}(\mathbb{R})})

(A.20)

To deal with
I_{2}
we apply (
A.18
) to deduce

|I_{2}(y)|\leqslant\varepsilon\int_{|x|>N/\lambda}|x|^{-\alpha}|h_{j}(y-x)|\,dx\leqslant\varepsilon\int_{\mathbb{R}}|x|^{-\alpha}|h_{j}(y-x)|\,dx.

Thus, the Hardy–Littlewood–Sobolev inequality on
\mathbb{R}
with
1/p^{\prime\prime}=1+1/p-\alpha

[
BCD11
, Theorem 1.7]
implies

\|I_{2}\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R})}\leqslant\varepsilon\|h_{j}\|_{L_{p^{\prime\prime}}(\mathbb{R})}\leqslant\varepsilon 2^{j(\alpha-1/p)}(\|h\|_{L_{p^{\prime\prime}}(\mathbb{R})}+\|h_{-1}\|_{L_{p^{\prime\prime}}(\mathbb{R})}).

Combining this with (
A
) and (
A.20
), we finally get

\|f_{\lambda}-\chi\|_{\mathcal{B}^{-\alpha-\beta+1/p}_{p}}\leqslant C_{N}\lambda^{-\beta}(\|h\|_{L_{p^{\prime}}(\mathbb{R})}+\|h_{-1}\|_{L_{p^{\prime}}(\mathbb{R})})+\varepsilon(\|h\|_{L_{p^{\prime\prime}}(\mathbb{R})}+\|h_{-1}\|_{L_{p^{\prime\prime}}(\mathbb{R})}).

Taking now first
\lambda\to\infty
, and recalling then that
\varepsilon
was arbitrary completes the proof.
∎

Appendix B
Other auxiliary results

Proposition B.1
.

Let
\Lambda
be a set and let
(X_{n,\lambda})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}_{+},\lambda\in\Lambda}
be a collection of random elements taking values in a metric space
E
. Let
(Y_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}}
be a collection of random elements taking values in a metric space
\widetilde{E}
. Suppose that
for each fixed
n
the random element
Y_{n}
is independent of
(X_{n,\lambda})_{\lambda\in\Lambda}
. Furthermore, assume that for each fixed
\lambda\in\Lambda
one has
X_{n,\lambda}\to X_{\lambda}
and
Y_{n}\to Y
in probability as
n\to\infty
. Then
Y
is independent of
(X_{\lambda})_{\lambda\in\Lambda}
.

Proof.

Consider a collection of
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\ldots,\lambda_{n}
for some
n\geq 1
. Then we can construct a common subsequence such that

X_{n_{k},\lambda_{j}}\to X_{\lambda_{j}}\mbox{ and }Y_{n_{k}}\to Y\mbox{ almost surely as $k\to\infty$ for all $1\leq j\leq n$.}

Let
h_{1},h_{2}\ldots h_{n}:E\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
,
g:\tilde{E}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
be bounded continuous functions. Then by the Lebesgue dominated convergence theorem,

\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt(\prod_{j=1}^{n}h_{j}(X_{n_{k},\lambda_{j}})g(Y_{n_{k}}))\to\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt(\prod_{j=1}^{n}h_{j}(X_{\lambda_{j}})g(Y))\mbox{ as $k\to\infty$ }

and also

\prod_{j=1}^{n}\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt(h_{j}(X_{n_{k},\lambda_{j}}))\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057ptg(Y_{n_{k}})\to\prod_{j=1}^{n}\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt(h_{j}(X_{\lambda_{j}}))\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt(g(Y))\mbox{ as $k\to\infty$ }.

We have assumed for each
k
the random element
Y_{n_{k}}
is independent of
(X_{n_{k},\lambda_{j}})_{1\leq j\leq n}
. Therefore from the above it is immediate that

\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt\prod_{j=1}^{n}(h_{j}(X_{\lambda_{j}}))g(Y))=\prod_{j=1}^{n}\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt(h_{j}(X_{\lambda_{j}}))\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt(g(Y))

As,
n\geq 1
,
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\ldots,\lambda_{n}
and
h_{1},h_{2}\ldots h_{n},g
were arbitrary, the result follows.
∎

Lemma B.2

(Gaussian process representation)
.

Let
(\widetilde{\Omega},\widetilde{\mathcal{F}},\widetilde{\mathsf{P}})
be a filtered probability space. Let
\widetilde{V}\colon[0,T_{0}]\times{D}\times\widetilde{\Omega}\to\mathbb{R}
be a measurable function with the same law as
V
.
Then on the same space there exists a white noise
\widetilde{W}
such that identity (
2.4
) holds with
\widetilde{V}
in place of
V
and
\widetilde{W}
in place of
W
. Furthermore,

1.

\mathcal{F}_{t}^{\widetilde{W}}=\mathcal{F}_{t}^{\widetilde{V}}
for any
t\in[0,T_{0}]
;

2.

suppose additionally that there exists a filtration
(\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{t})_{t\in[0,T_{0}]}
such that
\mathcal{F}_{t}^{\widetilde{V}}\subset\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{t}
and for any
(s,t)\in\Delta_{0,T_{0}}
,
\varphi\in\mathcal{C}_{c}^{\infty}
the random variable
\int_{{D}}(\widetilde{V}_{t}(x)-P_{t-s}\widetilde{V}_{s}(x))\varphi(x)\,dx
is independent of
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{s}
. Then
\widetilde{W}
is
(\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{t})
-white noise.

Proof.

The result is probably well-known. However, we give a proof for the sake of completeness. In what follows we will use the following notation:

\langle f,g\rangle:=\int_{D}f(y)g(y)\,dy,

for measurable functions
f,g\colon{D}\to\mathbb{R}
for which the above integral is well-defined.
It is well-known that (
2.4
) is equivalent (see, e.g.,
[
Shi94
, Theorem 2.1]
) to representing
V
as a solution to the additive stochastic heat equation in a distributional form:

\langle V_{t},\varphi\rangle=\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{t}\left\langle V_{s},\partial^{2}_{yy}\varphi\right\rangle\,ds+W_{t}(\varphi),\quad\text{for any $t\geq 0$, $\varphi\in\mathcal{C}_{c}^{\infty}$}.

(B.1)

Since
\widetilde{V}
has the same law as
V
, we immediately get that the functional

\widetilde{W}_{t}(\varphi):=\langle\widetilde{V}_{t},\varphi\rangle-\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{t}\left\langle\widetilde{V}_{s},\partial^{2}_{yy}\varphi\right\rangle\,ds,\quad t\geq 0,\varphi\in\mathcal{C}_{c}^{\infty}

(B.2)

has the same distributional properties as
W
. That is, for any
\varphi\in\mathcal{C}_{c}^{\infty}
, the process
(\widetilde{W}_{t}(\varphi))_{t\in[0,T_{0}]}
is an
(\mathcal{F}^{\widetilde{V}}_{t})
–Brownian motion with
\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt\widetilde{W}_{1}(\varphi)^{2}=\|\varphi\|^{2}_{L_{2}({D},dx)}

and clearly this also holds for any

\varphi\in{L_{2}}({D},dx)
since
\mathcal{C}_{c}^{\infty}
is dense in

L^{2}({D},dx)
. Also
\widetilde{W}_{t}(\varphi)
and
\widetilde{W}_{t}(\psi)
are independent whenever

\varphi,\psi\in\mathcal{C}_{c}^{\infty}
with
\int_{D}\varphi(x)\psi(x)\,dx=0
, and again since
\mathcal{C}_{c}^{\infty}
is dense in

L^{2}({D},dx)
, this holds for any

\varphi,\psi\in{L_{2}}({D},dx)
with
\int_{D}\varphi(x)\psi(x)\,dx=0
. This immediately
implies that
\widetilde{W}
is an
(\mathcal{F}^{\widetilde{V}}_{t})
-white noise. Thus,
\widetilde{V}
is a solution to

\langle\widetilde{V}_{t},\varphi\rangle=\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{t}\left\langle\widetilde{V}_{s},\partial^{2}_{yy}\varphi\right\rangle\,ds+\widetilde{W}_{t}(\varphi),\quad\text{for any $t\geq 0$, $\varphi\in\mathcal{C}_{c}^{\infty}$},

(B.3)

with white noise
\widetilde{W}
. Using again
[
Shi94
, Theorem 2.1]
,
we get that (
2.4
) holds with
\widetilde{V}
in place of
V
and
\widetilde{W}
in place of
W
.

To prove (i) note that

\widetilde{V}_{t}(x)=\int_{0}^{t}\int_{{D}}p_{t-r}(x,y)\widetilde{W}(dr,dy),\quad t\geqslant 0,\,x\in{D},

(B.4)

and thus for any
t\in[0,T_{0}]
,
\mathcal{F}_{t}^{\widetilde{V}}\subset\mathcal{F}_{t}^{\widetilde{W}}
.
On the other hand, from (
B.2
) we also immediately get that

\mathcal{F}_{t}^{\widetilde{W}}\subset\mathcal{F}_{t}^{\widetilde{V}}
for any
t\in[0,T_{0}],
and (i) follows.

Let us prove part (ii) of the proposition.
We need just to show that for any
(s,t)\in\Delta_{0,T_{0}}
,
\varphi\in L^{2}({D},dx)

\widetilde{W}_{t}(\varphi)-\widetilde{W}_{s}(\varphi)\;\text{is independent of}\;\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{s}\,.

Fix arbitrary
(s,t)\in\Delta_{0,T_{0}}
and
\varphi\in\mathcal{C}_{c}^{\infty}
.
By (
2.4
) and stochastic Fubini theorem we get that

\int_{{D}}(\widetilde{V}_{t}(x)-P_{t-s}\widetilde{V}_{s}(x))\varphi(x)\,dx=\int_{s}^{t}\int_{D}P_{t-r}\varphi(y)\widetilde{W}(dr,dy).

By our assumptions, we get that the above stochastic integral is independent of
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{s}
.
Clearly, since
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{s}\subset\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{r}
for any
s\leqslant r
, we get that for any
(s_{1},s_{2})\in\Delta_{s,t}
,

Y_{s_{1},s_{2}}:=\int_{s_{1}}^{s_{2}}\int_{D}P_{s_{2}-r}\varphi(y)\widetilde{W}(dr,dy)\;\text{is independent of $\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{s}$.}

(B.5)

For any
n\geq 1
define
s^{n}_{k}:=s+\frac{(t-s)k}{n}
for
k=0,\ldots,n

and a function
f^{n}
on
[s,t]\times{D}
such that

f^{n}(r,y)=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\1_{s^{n}_{k-1}\leq r<s^{n}_{k}}P_{s^{n}_{k}-r}\varphi(y),\;s\leq r\leq t,y\in{D}.

Now we are ready to define the sequence of random variables

Y^{n}:=\sum_{k=1}^{n}Y_{s^{n}_{k-1},s^{n}_{k}}=\int_{s}^{t}\int_{D}f^{n}(r,y)\widetilde{W}(dr,dy).

It is trivial to check that

f^{n}\rightarrow\varphi,\;\text{in}\;L^{2}([s,t]\times{D}),\;\text{as $n\rightarrow\infty$},

and hence

Y^{n}\rightarrow\int_{s}^{t}\int_{D}\varphi(y)\widetilde{W}(dr,dy)=\widetilde{W}_{t}(\varphi)-\widetilde{W}_{s}(\varphi),\;\text{as $n\rightarrow\infty$},

where convergence is in
L^{2}(\Omega)
.
By (
B.5
) and properties of the white noise we get that
Y^{n}
is independent of
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{s}
for all
n
, and
hence the limit in
L^{2}(\Omega)
of this sequence, is also independent
of
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{s}
. Thus we get that
\widetilde{W}_{t}(\varphi)-\widetilde{W}_{s}(\varphi)

is independent
of
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{s}
, for any
\varphi\in\mathcal{C}_{c}^{\infty}.

We can easily get the same property for any

\varphi\in L^{2}({D},dx)
, by approximating such
\varphi
in
L^{2}({D},dx)

by a sequence of functions
\varphi^{n}\in\mathcal{C}_{c}^{\infty}

and again passing to the limit of corresponding sequence of random variables
\widetilde{W}_{t}(\varphi_{n})-\widetilde{W}_{s}(\varphi_{n})
.
∎

Lemma B.3
.

For every
\alpha,\beta\in[0,1)
,
(s,t)\in\Delta_{S,T}
and
u=(s+t)/2
, we have

(u-S)^{-\alpha}(T-u)^{-\beta}(t-s)\leqslant 2^{1+\alpha}\int_{s}^{t}(r-S)^{-\alpha}(T-r)^{-\beta}dr

(B.6)

and

\int_{s}^{t}(T-r)^{-\beta}(r-s)^{-\alpha}\,dr\leqslant C(T-s)^{-\beta}(t-s)^{1-\alpha}.

(B.7)

Proof.

We have

\displaystyle\int_{s}^{t}(r-S)^{-\alpha}(T-r)^{-\beta}dr\geqslant\int_{u}^{t}(r-S)^{-\alpha}(T-r)^{-\beta}dr\geqslant(t-S)^{-\alpha}(T-u)^{-\beta}(t-u).

Since
u=(s+t)/2
,
t-S\leqslant 2(u-S)
, we obtain (
B.6
) from the above inequalities.

For fixed
s\leqslant t
, the function
q(T):=\int_{s}^{t}\left(\frac{T-s}{T-r}\right)^{\beta}(r-s)^{-\alpha}dr
is decreasing on
T\in[t,\infty)
. Hence we have
q(T)\leqslant q(t)=\left(\int_{0}^{1}(1-r)^{-\beta}r^{-\alpha}dr\right)(t-s)^{1-\alpha}
. This yields (
B.7
).
∎

Lemma B.4
.

Let
f:\Omega\times{D}\to\mathbb{R}
be a measurable function. Then for any
s,t\geqslant 0
,
P_{t}f:\Omega\times{D}\to\mathbb{R}
is measurable. Further for any
x\in{D}
,
m\in[1,\infty]
,
n\in[1,\infty]
, and a
\sigma
-algebra
\mathscr{G}
we have

\displaystyle\|\|P_{t}f(x)\|_{{L_{m}}|\mathscr{G}}\|_{{L_{n}}}\leqslant\sup_{y\in{D}}\|\,\|f(y)\|_{{L_{m}}|\mathscr{G}}\|_{{L_{n}}}\quad\textrm{and}\quad\|P_{t}f(x)\|_{{L_{m}}}\leqslant\sup_{y\in{D}}\|f(y)\|_{{L_{m}}}.

(B.8)

In addition, if
f\in{\mathbf{B}L_{m}}
, there exists a set
\Omega^{\prime}\subset\Omega
of full measure such that for any
\omega\in\Omega^{\prime}

P_{t}f(x,\omega)<\infty\quad\text{for Lebesgue almost every $(t,x)\in[0,T_{0}]\times{D}$}.

(B.9)

Proof.

It suffices to show the result assuming that
f
is non-negative.
In such case, it is evident that
P_{t}f:\Omega\times\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
is measurable.
Applying the conditional integral Minkowski inequality, we obtain that

\|P_{t}f(x)\|_{{L_{m}}|\mathscr{G}}\leqslant\int_{D}p_{t}(x,y)\|f(y)\|_{{L_{m}}|\mathscr{G}}\,dy.

We then apply Minkowski inequality, to get

\|\|P_{t}f(x)\|_{{L_{m}}|\mathscr{G}}\|_{L_{n}}\leqslant\int_{D}p_{t}(x,y)\|\|f(y)\|_{{L_{m}}|\mathscr{G}}\|_{L_{n}}\leqslant\sup_{y\in{D}}\|\|f(y)\|_{{L_{m}}|\mathscr{G}}\|_{L_{n}}\,dy

which implies the former estimate in (
B.8
). The later one in (
B.8
) follows from the former by setting
n=m
.

Finally, (
B.9
) follows from (
B.8
) and the Fubini theorem.
∎

Lemma B.5
.

Let
(\varphi^{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}}
be a sequence of continuous random fields on
{D}
such that
\varphi^{n}
converges to
0
in
\mathcal{C}_{\textrm{uc}}({D})
in probability and that
\sup_{n\in\mathbb{Z}_{+},x\in{D}}\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt|\varphi^{n}(x)|<\infty
. Then for every
(t,x)\in[0,T_{0}]\times{D}
,
P_{t}\varphi^{n}(x)
converges to
0
in probability as
n\to\infty
.

Proof.

For every
M>0
, we have

\displaystyle|P_{t}\varphi^{n}(x)|

\displaystyle\leqslant\int_{|y|\leqslant M}p_{t}(x,y)|\varphi^{n}(y)|dy+\int_{|y|>M}p_{t}(x,y)|\varphi^{n}(y)|dy

\displaystyle\leqslant\sup_{|y|\leqslant M}|\varphi^{n}(y)|+\int_{|y|>M}p_{t}(x,y)|\varphi^{n}(y)|dy.

Hence, for every
\varepsilon>0
, we have by Chebyshev inequality,

\displaystyle\mathsf{P}\left(|P_{t}\varphi^{n}(x)|>2\varepsilon\right)\leqslant\mathsf{P}(\sup_{|y|\leqslant M}|\varphi^{n}(y)|>\varepsilon)+\varepsilon^{-1}\sup_{n,z}\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt|\varphi^{n}(z)|\left(\int_{|y|>M}p_{t}(x,y)dy\right).

We send
n\to\infty
then
M\to\infty
to see that
\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathsf{P}\left(|P_{t}\varphi^{n}(x)|>2\varepsilon\right)=0
. This implies the result.
∎

Appendix C
Heat kernel estimates

In this section we provide a number of standard simple statements characterizing smoothing properties of the heat kernel. Recall that
G
and
P
are the heat semigroups on
\mathbb{R}
and
{D}
defined in (
2.1
) and
2.1
, correspondingly.

Lemma C.1
.

For
(D,p)\in\{(\mathbb{R},g),({[0,1]},p^{per}),({[0,1]},p^{Neu})\}
, any
t\in[0,T_{0}]
,
x\in{D}
we have

\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}t^{-1/2}\leqslant p_{t}(x,x)\leqslant C(T_{0})+\frac{2}{\sqrt{2\pi}}t^{-1/2}.

(C.1)

Proof.

Since
g_{t}(0)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi t}}
, and
p_{t}(x,x)\geqslant g_{t}(0)
for
p\in\{g,p^{per},p^{Neu}\}
, the lower bound in (
C.1
) is trivial.
We apply the elementary inequality
e^{-|z|}\leqslant c|z|^{-1}
to get

\displaystyle p^{per}_{t}(x,x)

\displaystyle=g_{t}(0)+\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}:|n|\geqslant 1}g_{t}(n)\leqslant g_{t}(0)+c\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}:|n|\geqslant 1}t^{\frac{1}{2}}n^{-2}

which shows the upper bound in (
C.1
) for
p=p^{per}
.
For
p^{Neu}
, using the estimate
g_{t}(2x+2n)\leqslant g_{t}(2n)
for
x\in[0,1]
, we have

\displaystyle p^{Neu}_{t}(x,x)\leqslant 2g_{t}(0)+2\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}:|n|\geqslant 1}g_{t}(2n).

From here, using the same argument as above, we obtain the upper bound in (
C.1
) for
p=p^{Neu}
.
∎

Lemma C.2
.

For every
\alpha\in[0,1]
, there exists a constant
C=C(\alpha,T_{0})>0
such that for any
s,t\in(0,T_{0}]
,
s\leqslant t
,
x,x_{1},x_{2}\in{D}
we have

\displaystyle\int_{{D}}|p_{t}(x_{1},y)-p_{t}(x_{2},y)|\,dy\leqslant C|x_{1}-x_{2}|^{\alpha}t^{-\alpha/2},

(C.2)

\displaystyle\int_{{D}}p_{t}(x,y)|y-x|^{\alpha}\,dy\leqslant Ct^{\alpha/2},

(C.3)

\displaystyle\int_{D}|p_{t}(x,y)-p_{s}(x,y)|dy\leqslant Cs^{-\alpha/2}(t-s)^{\alpha/2}.

(C.4)

Proof.

From the elementary estimate

|g_{t}(x_{1}-y)-g_{t}(x_{2}-y)|\leqslant C|x_{1}-x_{2}|^{\alpha}t^{-\alpha/2}(g_{2t}(x_{1}-y)+g_{2t}(x_{2}-y))

and (
2.2
) and (
2.3
),
we obtain that

\displaystyle|p_{t}(x_{1},y)-p_{t}(x_{2},y)|\leqslant C|x_{1}-x_{2}|^{\alpha}t^{-\alpha/2}(p_{2t}(x_{1},y)+p_{2t}(x_{2},y))

for
p\in\{g,p^{per},p^{Neu}\}
.
Integrating over
y\in{D}
and note that
\int_{D}p_{2t}(x,y)dy=1
for each
x\in{D}
, we obtain (
C.2
) for
p\in\{g,p^{per},p^{Neu}\}
.

The estimate (
C.3
) for
p_{t}(x,y)=g_{t}(x-y)
follows easily by a change of variable. For the other cases, we note that
g_{t}(z+k)\leqslant cg_{t}(z)e^{-2\frac{|k|}{t}}
for every
|z|\leqslant 2
and every
k\in\mathbb{R}
. Since
\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}e^{-2\frac{|n|}{t}}\leqslant C(T_{0})
, we see that from (
2.2
) and (
2.3
) that

\displaystyle\int_{D}p_{t}(x,y)|y-x|^{\alpha}dy\leqslant C(T_{0})\int_{\mathbb{R}}g_{t}(x-y)|x-y|^{\alpha}dy.

From here, we obtain (
C.3
) for
p\in\{p^{per},p^{Neu}\}
by a change of variable.

To show (
C.4
), we use the estimate

\displaystyle\int_{D}|p_{t}(x,y)-p_{s}(x,y)|dy\leqslant\int_{D}\int_{D}p_{t-s}(x,z)|p_{s}(z,y)-p_{s}(x,y)|dydz

and (
C.2
) to obtain that

\displaystyle\int_{D}|p_{t}(x,y)-p_{s}(x,y)|dy\leqslant Cs^{-\alpha/2}\int_{D}p_{t-s}(x,z)|z-x|^{\alpha}dz.

Taking into account (
C.3
), we obtain (
C.4
) from the above inequality.
∎

Recall that
V
is defined in (
2.4
). For
t>0
,
x\in{D}
put

\rho_{t}(x):=\mathop{\mbox{\rm Var}}(V_{t}(x)).

(C.5)

Lemma C.3
.

For any
t\in[0,T_{0}]
,
x\in{D}
we have

\frac{\sqrt{t}}{\sqrt{\pi}}\leqslant{\rho_{t}}(x)\leqslant C(T_{0})\sqrt{t}.

(C.6)

Proof.

We have from (
C.1
)

{\rho_{t}}(x)=\mathop{\mbox{\rm Var}}(V(t,x))=\int_{0}^{t}p_{2(t-r)}(x,x)\,dr\geqslant\int_{0}^{t}\frac{1}{2\sqrt{\pi(t-r)}}\,dr=\frac{\sqrt{t}}{\sqrt{\pi}}.

The upper bound on
{\rho_{t}}
is established in exactly the same way.
∎

Lemma C.4
.

Let
0\leqslant s\leqslant u\leqslant t\leqslant T_{0}
.
Let
f\colon\mathbb{R}\times\Omega\to\mathbb{R}
be a bounded
\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{R})\otimes\mathcal{F}_{s}
-measurable function. Then

\displaystyle\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{u}f(V_{t}(x))=G_{\rho_{t-u}(x)}f(P_{t-u}V_{u}(x)).

(C.7)

In addition, there exists a universal constant
C=C(T_{0})>0
such that for every
x\in{D}
,
\gamma<0
,
n\in[1,\infty]
and
p\in[n,\infty]

\displaystyle\|\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{u}f(V_{t}(x))\|_{{L_{n}}}\leqslant C\|\,\|f\|_{\mathcal{B}^{\gamma}_{p}}\|_{{L_{n}}}(t-u)^{\frac{\gamma}{4}}(u-s)^{-\frac{1}{2p}}(t-s)^{\frac{1}{4p}},

(C.8)

\displaystyle|\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s}f(V_{t}(x))|\leqslant C\|f\|_{\mathcal{B}^{\gamma}_{p}}(t-s)^{\frac{\gamma}{4}-\frac{1}{4p}}.

(C.9)

Proof.

For
s\leqslant t
introduce the process

Z_{s,t}(x):=V_{t}(x)-P_{t-s}V_{s}(x)=\int_{s}^{t}\int_{D}p_{t-r}(x,y)W(dr,dy),\quad x\in{D},\,0\leqslant s\leqslant t.

By definition of
Z
, we have for any
0\leqslant s\leqslant u\leqslant t
,
x\in{D}

V_{t}(x)=P_{t-u}V_{u}(x)+Z_{u,t}(x).

(C.10)

It is immediate to see that
Z_{u,t}(x)
is independent of
\mathcal{F}_{s}
and is Gaussian with zero mean and variance

\mathop{\mbox{\rm Var}}(Z_{u,t}(x))=\int_{u}^{t}p_{2(t-r)}(x,x)dr=\rho_{t-u}(x).

(C.11)

Using (
C.10
), this yields

\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{u}f(V_{t}(x))(\omega)=[G_{\rho_{t-u}(x)}f(\cdot,\omega)](P_{t-u}V_{u}(x)),

which is (
C.7
).

Next, we show (
C.8
). To proceed, we further decompose
P_{t-u}V_{u}(x)=P_{t-s}V_{s}(x)+P_{t-u}Z_{s,u}(x)
. The random variable
P_{t-u}Z_{s,u}(x)=\int_{s}^{u}\int_{D}p_{t-r}(x,y)W(dr,dy)
is independent of
\mathcal{F}_{s}
and has a Gaussian law with mean zero and variance

\displaystyle\rho_{s,u,t}(x)

\displaystyle:=\mathop{\mbox{\rm Var}}[P_{t-u}Z_{s,u}(x)]=\int_{s}^{u}p_{2(t-r)}(x,x)\,dr\geqslant C(\sqrt{t-s}-\sqrt{t-u})

\displaystyle\geqslant C(u-s)(t-s)^{-1/2},

(C.12)

where the first inequality follows from (
C.1
).
Hence, using (
C.7
) and the fact that
f
is
\mathcal{F}_{s}
-measurable, we have

\displaystyle\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s}|\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{u}f(V_{t}(x))|^{n}

\displaystyle=\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s}\bigl[|G_{\rho_{t-u}(x)}f(P_{t-u}V_{u}(x))|^{n}\bigr]

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}}g_{\rho_{s,u,t}(x)}(z)\left|G_{\rho_{t-u}(x)}f(P_{t-s}V_{s}(x)+z)\right|^{n}\,dz,

(C.13)

where
g
is the standard Gaussian density (
2.1
).
Now we put
q=\frac{p}{n}\geqslant 1
,
\frac{1}{q}+\frac{1}{q^{\prime}}=1
and apply Hölder inequality and (
C
) to estimate the above integral from above by

\Bigl[\int_{\mathbb{R}}\bigl|G_{\rho_{t-u}(x)}f(P_{t-s}V_{s}(x)+z)\bigr|^{p}\,dz\Bigr]^{n/p}\,\,\|g_{\rho_{s,u,t}(x)}(\cdot)\|_{L^{q^{\prime}}},

(C.14)

The first factor in the above expression equals

\|G_{\rho_{t-u}(x)}f(\cdot,\omega)\|^{n}_{{L_{p}}(\mathbb{R})}
and thus is bounded above by

C\|f(\cdot,\omega)\|_{\mathcal{B}^{\gamma}_{p}}^{n}\rho_{t-u}(x)^{\frac{\gamma n}{2}}

by
Lemma

A.3
(i).
The second factor in (
C.14
) is bounded by
C\rho_{s,u,t}(x)^{-\frac{n}{2p}}
for some constant
C>0
, where we again used
Lemma

A.3
(i) for Dirac delta. Taking into account (
C.6
), the lower bound for
\rho_{s,u,t}(x)
in (
C
), we continue (
C
) as follows:

\displaystyle\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s}|\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{u}f(V_{t}(x))|^{n}

\displaystyle\leqslant C\|f(\cdot,\omega)\|_{\mathcal{B}^{\gamma}_{p}}^{n}\rho_{t-u}(x)^{\frac{\gamma n}{2}}\rho_{s,u,t}(x)^{-\frac{n}{2p}}

\displaystyle\leqslant C(T_{0})\|f(\cdot,\omega)\|_{\mathcal{B}^{\gamma}_{p}}^{n}(t-u)^{\frac{\gamma n}{4}}(u-s)^{-\frac{n}{2p}}(t-s)^{\frac{n}{4p}}.

Taking expectation, we get (
C.8
).

To show (
C.9
), we use (
C.7
) with
u=s
. Applying
Lemma

A.3
(i) and the Besov embedding
\mathcal{B}^{\gamma}_{p}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{B}^{\gamma-1/p}_{\infty}
(see (
3.6
)), we deduce

|\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s}f(V_{t}(x))|\leqslant C\|f\|_{\mathcal{B}^{\gamma-1/p}_{\infty}}\rho_{t-s}(x)^{\frac{\gamma}{2}-\frac{1}{2p}}\leqslant C\|f\|_{\mathcal{B}^{\gamma}_{p}}(t-s)^{\frac{\gamma}{4}-\frac{1}{4p}},

where we also used (
C.6
). This implies (
C.9
).
∎
